is a pullback of cartesian 2-categories along cartesian maps, it is cartesian.∎

3.2
Loose bimodules as pseudo-bimodules

Let’s see how the definition of
loose bimodule
given in
Definition
3.3
relates to perhaps more expected definition of a pseudo-bimodule acted on the left and right by pseudo-categories in
\mathcal{C}\mathsf{at}
. We will only sketch the equivalence here; a full proof will appear in
[
BCLJ25
]
.

First, we note that to any
loose bimodule
, we may associate a category which we call its
carrier
.

Definition 3.13

(Carrier of a loose bimodule)
.

Let
\ell:\mathbb{M}\to\mathbb{L}\mathsf{oose}
be a loose bimodule. Its carrier
\mathsf{Car}(\mathbb{M})
is the category whose:

•

Objects are loose morphisms of
\mathbb{M}
living over the walking loose arrow. We refer to such loose morphisms as
heteromorphisms
.

•

Morphisms are squares of
\mathbb{M}
living over the identity square of the walking loose arrow.

Writing the loose bimodule
\mathbb{M}\to\mathbb{L}\mathsf{oose}
as a
proarrow between its source and target

\mathbb{M}\colon\mathbb{D}_{0}\mathrel{\mkern 3.0mu\vbox{\hbox{$\shortmid$}}\mkern-10.0mu{\to}}\mathbb{D}_{1}

we have the following explication.

An object of
\mathsf{Car}(\mathbb{M})
consists of a triple

(d_{0},d_{1},m\colon d_{0}\mathrel{\mkern 3.0mu\vbox{\hbox{$\shortmid$}}\mkern-10.0mu{\to}}d_{1})

where
d_{0}
is an object of
\mathbb{D}_{0}
,
d_{1}
is an object of
\mathbb{D}_{1}
, and
m
is a loose morphism in
\mathbb{M}
from
d_{0}
to
d_{1}
.

A morphism
(d_{0},d_{1},m\colon d_{0}\mathrel{\mkern 3.0mu\vbox{\hbox{$\shortmid$}}\mkern-10.0mu{\to}}d_{1})\to(d_{0}^{\prime},d_{1}^{\prime},m^{\prime}\colon d_{0}^{\prime}\mathrel{\mkern 3.0mu\vbox{\hbox{$\shortmid$}}\mkern-10.0mu{\to}}d_{1}^{\prime})
consists of a tight morphism
f_{0}\colon d_{0}\to d_{0}^{\prime}
in
\mathbb{D}_{0}
, a tight morphism
f_{1}\colon d_{1}\to d_{1}^{\prime}
in the
\mathbb{D}_{0}
, and a square
\alpha\colon m\Rightarrow m^{\prime}
in
\mathbb{M}
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{d_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{d_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{d_{0}^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{d_{1}^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}

The
carrier
of a
loose bimodule
is spanned over the category of tight arrows of its
source and target
.

Proposition 3.14

(Projection from the carrier of a loose bimodule to its source and target)
.

Given a
loose bimodule

\mathbb{M}\colon\mathbb{D}_{0}\mathrel{\mkern 3.0mu\vbox{\hbox{$\shortmid$}}\mkern-10.0mu{\to}}\mathbb{D}_{1}
, there are projections from its
carrier
to the tight category of its
source and target
of
\mathbb{M}
. This gives a span:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{Car}(\mathbb{M})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{Tight}({\mathbb{D}_{0}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{Tight}({\mathbb{D}_{1}})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{dom}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{codom}}

With regard to these projections, the carrier
\mathsf{Car}(\mathbb{M})
picks up left and right actions by
\mathbb{D}_{0}
and
\mathbb{D}_{1}
by composition in
\mathbb{M}
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{Tight}({\mathbb{D}_{0}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{Tight}({\mathbb{D}_{0}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{Tight}({\mathbb{D}_{1}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{Tight}({\mathbb{D}_{1}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{Loose}({\mathbb{D}_{0}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{Car}(\mathbb{M})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{Loose}({\mathbb{D}_{1}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{Car}(\mathbb{M})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{Tight}({\mathbb{D}_{0}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{Tight}({\mathbb{D}_{1}})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{dom}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{codom}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

We will often find it more convenient to think of
loose bimodules

\mathbb{M}
as being displayed categories
\mathsf{Car}(\mathbb{M})
acted on the left and right by double categories. This will make the upcoming constructions of
restriction
and
collapse
more easy to understand as well.

Let’s now sketch the relationship between
loose bimodules
presented as double barrels (
Definition
3.3
) and as pseudo-bimodules between pseudo-categories in
\mathcal{C}\mathsf{at}
; this will be a sneak peak at
[
BCLJ25
]
, though we do not follow exactly this strategy there.

As we saw in
Definition
2.4
, we may identify the 2-category
\mathcal{D}\mathsf{bl}
of double categories with the 2-category
\mathcal{M}\mathsf{od}_{p}(\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{PseudoCat}};\mathcal{C}\mathsf{at})
of models of the
\mathcal{F}
-sketch of pseudo-categories valued in the 2-category
\mathcal{C}\mathsf{at}
of categories. We may consider the strict double category
\mathbb{L}\mathsf{oose}:\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{PseudoCat}}\to\mathcal{C}\mathsf{at}
as such a model; it’s 2-category of elements
\mathcal{E}\mathsf{l}(\mathbb{L}\mathsf{oose})
may be endowed with the structure of an
\mathcal{F}
-sketch by taking its marked cones to be those that project down to marked cones in
\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{PseudoCat}}
.

An inspection of
\mathcal{E}\mathsf{l}(\mathbb{L}\mathsf{oose})
reveals that it gives a
\mathcal{F}
-sketch for pseudo-bimodules between pseudo-categories. By adapting closure properties of 2-fibrations to
\mathcal{F}
-categories, we can prove a categorification of the usual theorem relating slice categories of presheaf categories to presheaves on categories of elements:

\mathcal{M}\mathsf{od}_{p}(\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{PseudoCat}};\mathcal{C}\mathsf{at})\downarrow\mathbb{L}\mathsf{oose}\simeq\mathcal{M}\mathsf{od}_{p}(\mathcal{E}\mathsf{l}(\mathbb{L}\mathsf{oose});\mathcal{C}\mathsf{at}).

This gives the equivalence between the definition of loose bimodule we gave in
Definition
3.3
and pseudo-bimodules between pseudo-categories.

3.3
Collapse and restriction of loose bimodules

In this section, we’ll sketch two constructions of new loose bimodules from old:
collapse
and
restriction
. All of the loose modules of systems we construct in this paper will be done by restricting
loose hom bimodules
, and then potentially collapsing them.

We’ll begin with the collapse, since it is more straightforward. We then describe the restriction of loose bimodules, and in particular its pseudo-functoriality over a 2-category of
niches
.

3.3.1
Collapse of loose bimodules

Given a
loose bimodule

\mathbb{M}:\mathbb{D}_{0}\mathrel{\mkern 3.0mu\vbox{\hbox{$\shortmid$}}\mkern-10.0mu{\to}}\mathbb{D}_{1}
we may consider it as a pseudo-bimodule
spanned between its source
\mathbb{D}_{0}
and its target
\mathbb{D}_{1}
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{Car}(\mathbb{M})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{Tight}({\mathbb{D}_{0}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{Tight}({\mathbb{D}_{1}})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{dom}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{codom}}

The
collapse

\mathfrak{c}\mathbb{M}
of
\mathbb{M}
is the
loose right module
given by replacing
\mathbb{D}_{0}
by the terminal double category:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{Car}(\mathbb{M})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{Tight}({\mathbb{D}_{1}})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces!}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{codom}}

In particular, the collapse
\mathfrak{c}\mathbb{M}
of a loose bimodule
\mathbb{M}
has exactly the same
carrier
.

This construction is a bit awkward to express with double barrels but evidently 2-functorial when viewed as a construction on pseudo-bimodules. We therefore have the following theorem:

Theorem 3.15

(Collapse of loose bimodule)
.

The
collapse
of a
loose bimodule
into a
loose right module
gives a cartesian 2-functor

\mathfrak{c}:{\ell}\mathcal{B}\mathsf{imod}\to{\ell}\mathcal{M}\mathsf{od}_{\mathsf{r}}.

3.3.2
Restriction of loose bimodules

In this section, we will sketch the
restriction
of loose bimodules along pseudo-double functors. The construction itself itself is rather straightforward. Suppose we are given a
loose bimodule

\mathbb{M}:\mathbb{D}_{0}\mathrel{\mkern 3.0mu\vbox{\hbox{$\shortmid$}}\mkern-10.0mu{\to}}\mathbb{D}_{1}
and two double functors
F_{0}:\mathbb{E}_{0}\to\mathbb{D}_{0}
and
F_{1}:\mathbb{E}_{1}\to\mathbb{D}_{1}
. We may then form the carrier of the restricted loose bimodule
\mathbb{M}(F_{0},F_{1})
as the limit

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{Tight}({\mathbb{E}_{0}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{Car}(\mathbb{M})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{Tight}({\mathbb{E}_{1}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{Tight}({\mathbb{D}_{0}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{Tight}({\mathbb{D}_{1}})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Tight}({F_{0}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{dom}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{codom}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Tight}({F_{1}})}

Explicitly, we will have
\mathsf{Car}(\mathbb{M}(F_{0},F_{1}))
defined as follows:

•

Objects are triples
(e_{0}:{\mathsf{ob}}(\mathbb{E}_{0}),e_{1}:{\mathsf{ob}}(\mathbb{E}_{1}),m\colon F_{0}e_{0}\mathrel{\mkern 3.0mu\vbox{\hbox{$\shortmid$}}\mkern-10.0mu{\to}}F_{1}e_{1}).

•

A morphism
(e_{0},e_{1},m)\to(e_{0}^{\prime},e_{1}^{\prime},m^{\prime})
is a triple
(f_{0}:e_{0}\to e_{0}^{\prime},f_{1}:e_{1}\to e_{1}^{\prime},\alpha)
where
\alpha
is a square in
\mathbb{M}
of the following form:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{0}e_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{1}e_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{0}e_{0}^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{1}e_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{0}f_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{1}f_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}

The action of
\mathbb{E}_{0}
and
\mathbb{E}_{1}
is by first applying
F_{i}
, and then acting in
\mathbb{M}
.

What is more interesting is the pseudo-functoriality of the above construction. We will need a very particular sort of pseudo-functoriality in a domain 2-category which we will describe now.

Definition 3.16

(The 2-category of niches)
.

Consider the following pullback of 2-categories:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{N}\mathsf{}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\ell}\mathcal{B}\mathsf{imod}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{2}\mathcal{C}\mathsf{at}^{c}([1],\mathcal{D}\mathsf{bl})^{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{D}\mathsf{bl}^{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{codom}}^{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

Here
\mathsf{2}\mathcal{C}\mathsf{at}^{c}
is the 3-category of 2-categories, 2-functors, colax natural transformations,
and modifications, so that
\mathsf{2}\mathcal{C}\mathsf{at}^{c}([1],\mathcal{D}\mathsf{bl})
is the 2-category of pseudo double functors and
colax-commuting squares. (This will be explicated below.)

We define the 2-category
\mathcal{N}\mathsf{iche}
to be the wide and locally full sub-2-category of
\mathcal{N}\mathsf{}

on those 1-morphisms whose component in
\mathsf{2}\mathcal{C}\mathsf{at}^{c}([1],\mathcal{D}\mathsf{bl})^{2}
lies in

\mathsf{2}\mathcal{C}\mathsf{at}^{\mathsf{colax}}(\Delta[1],\mathcal{D}\mathsf{bl})_{\mathsf{conj}}\times\mathsf{2}\mathcal{C}\mathsf{at}^{\mathsf{colax}}(\Delta[1],\mathcal{D}\mathsf{bl})_{\mathsf{comp}}
, where

\mathsf{2}\mathcal{C}\mathsf{at}^{\mathsf{colax}}(\Delta[1],\mathcal{D}\mathsf{bl})_{\mathsf{conj}}
(resp.
\mathsf{2}\mathcal{C}\mathsf{at}^{\mathsf{colax}}(\Delta[1],\mathcal{D}\mathsf{bl})_{\mathsf{comp}}
) is the .

We also define the 2-category
\mathcal{N}\mathsf{iche}^{\cong}
to be the sub-2-category of
\mathcal{N}\mathsf{iche}
consisting of all objects, but only the 1-cells for which the colaxators are isomorphisms (and all 2-cells).

Explication 3.17

(The 2-category
\mathcal{N}\mathsf{iche}
)
.

Explicitly:

1.

An object of
\mathcal{N}\mathsf{iche}
consists of a loose bimodule
M:\mathbb{M}\to\mathbb{L}\mathsf{oose}
together with two double functors
F_{0}:\mathbb{E}_{0}\to M_{0}
and
F_{1}:\mathbb{E}_{1}\to M_{1}
. We will call an object of
\mathcal{N}\mathsf{iche}
a
niche
, and can draw it in this way:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{E}_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{E}_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{M_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{M_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}

2.

A 1-cell of the above pullback consists of a map
a:M_{0}\to M_{1}
of modules and double functors
f_{i}\colon\mathbb{E}_{0i}\to\mathbb{E}_{1i}
, together with two colax-commuting squares of double functors of the following form:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{E}_{0i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{E}_{1i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{M_{0i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{M_{1i}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{0i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{1i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{f}_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}

where
\overline{f}_{0}
is a
companion commuter transformation
and
\overline{f}_{1}
is a
conjoint commuter transformation
.

3.

A 2-cell of the above pullback consists of tight transformations
\alpha:a_{0}\to a_{1}
, and
\phi_{i}:f_{0i}\to f_{1i}
so that the following equations hold:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{E}_{0i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{E}_{1i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{E}_{00}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{E}_{1i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{E}_{0i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{E}_{1i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{=}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{M_{0i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{M_{1i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{M_{0i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{M_{1i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{M_{0i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{M_{1i}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{0i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{1i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{0i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{0i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{1i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{f}_{1i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{1i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{f}_{0i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{0i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{0i}}

We can now express the pseudo-functoriality of restriction. Because we will make reference to the action of restriction on 1-cells, we include their description here. We remark again the a full proof will appear in our
[
BCLJ25
]
.

Theorem 3.18

(Restriction of loose bimodules is a pseudo-functor)
.

Restriction of loose bimodules extends to a cartesian pseudo-functor

\mathsf{Res}:\mathcal{N}\mathsf{iche}\to{\ell}\mathcal{B}\mathsf{imod}.

Proof.

We will only describe the construction itself, foregoing its proof to
[
BCLJ25
]
.

Proof.

Consider the following 1-cell
(f_{0},\bar{f}_{0},f_{1},\bar{f}_{1},a):(F_{00},F_{01},M_{0})\to(F_{10},F_{11},M_{1})
of
\mathcal{N}\mathsf{iche}
, which we’ll abusively denote as
a
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{E}_{00}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{E}_{01}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{M_{00}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{M_{01}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{E}_{10}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{E}_{11}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{M_{10}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{M_{11}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{00}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{01}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{10}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{11}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{f}_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{f}_{1}}

We define a loose bimodule map
\mathsf{Res}(a):\mathbb{M}_{0}(F_{00},F_{01})\to\mathbb{M}_{1}(F_{10},F_{11})
as follows:

1.

(Endpoints)
On the
\mathbb{E}_{0i}
, we take
\mathsf{Res}(a)
to be
f_{i}
.

2.

(Objects of carrier)
Given
(e_{00},e_{01},m_{0}:F_{00}e_{00}\mathrel{\mkern 3.0mu\vbox{\hbox{$\shortmid$}}\mkern-10.0mu{\to}}F_{01}e_{01})
in
\mathbb{M}_{0}(F_{00},F_{01})
, recall that we have the tight morphism
\bar{f}_{0}e_{00}:a_{0}F_{00}(e_{00})\to F_{10}f_{0}(e_{00})
,
which has a conjoint
(\overline{f}_{0}e_{00})^{<}
as part of the definition of
\mathcal{N}\mathsf{iche}
. Similarly,
\bar{f}_{1}e_{01}
has a companion
(\overline{f}_{1}e_{01})^{>}
. These loosenings will be used to modify

am_{0}:a_{0}F_{00}e_{00}\mathrel{\mkern 3.0mu\vbox{\hbox{$\shortmid$}}\mkern-10.0mu{\to}}a_{1}F_{01}e_{01}
to have the correct signature.

We therefore define
\mathsf{Res}(a)(e_{00},e_{01},m_{0})
as
(f_{0}(e_{00}),f_{1}(e_{01}),m_{0}^{\prime})
where
m_{0}^{\prime}:F_{01}f_{0}(e_{00})\mathrel{\mkern 3.0mu\vbox{\hbox{$\shortmid$}}\mkern-10.0mu{\to}}F_{11}f_{1}(e_{01})
is the following composite in
\mathbb{M}_{1}
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{10}f_{0}e_{00}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a_{0}F_{00}e_{00}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a_{1}F_{01}e_{01}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{11}f_{1}e_{01}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\overline{f}_{0}e_{00})^{<}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces am_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\overline{f}_{1}e_{10})^{>}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}

3.

(Morphisms of carrier)
Consider a square
(\mu_{0},\mu_{1},\mu):(e_{00},e_{01},m_{0})\to(e_{00}^{\prime},e_{01}^{\prime},m_{0}^{\prime})
in
\mathbb{M}_{0}(F_{00},F_{01})
,
where
\mu
appears as below:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{00}e_{00}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{01}e_{01}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{00}e_{00}^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{01}e_{01}^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{00}\mu_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{01}\mu_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu}

Then we define
\mathsf{Res}(a)(\mu)=(f_{0}\mu_{0},f_{1}\mu_{1},\mu^{\prime})
where
\mu^{\prime}
is the following square:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{10}f_{0}e_{00}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a_{0}F_{00}e_{00}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a_{1}F_{01}e_{01}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{11}f_{1}e_{01}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{10}f_{0}e_{00}^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a_{0}F_{00}e_{00}^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a_{1}F_{01}e_{01}^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{11}f_{1}e_{01}^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\overline{f}_{0}e_{00})^{<}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{10}f_{0}\mu_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces am_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{0}F_{00}\mu_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\overline{f}_{0}e_{00})^{>}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{1}F_{01}\mu_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{11}f_{1}\mu_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\overline{f}_{0}e_{00}^{\prime})^{<}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces am_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\overline{f}_{0}e_{00})^{>}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\bar{f}_{0,\mu_{0}})^{>}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a\mu}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\bar{f}_{1,\mu_{1}})^{>}}

where the left (resp. right) square is the conjoint (resp. companion) transpose of the tight naturality square associated to
\overline{f}_{0}\mu_{0}
(resp.
\overline{f}_{1}\mu_{1}
).

4.

(Unitors and laxators)
The unit and lax structure is taken from
f_{i}
when in
\mathbb{E}_{0i}
.
There are two remaining cases for the laxators: when we are composing a loose in
\mathbb{E}_{0i}
with an
(e_{00},e_{01},m_{0}:F_{00}e_{00}\mathrel{\mkern 3.0mu\vbox{\hbox{$\shortmid$}}\mkern-10.0mu{\to}}F_{01}e_{01})
on one side or the other.
By symmetry, it is enough to consider the case of
\ell:e_{00}^{\prime}\mathrel{\mkern 3.0mu\vbox{\hbox{$\shortmid$}}\mkern-10.0mu{\to}}e_{00}
.
Then we have
\mathsf{Res}(a)(\ell)=f_{0}(\ell),\mathsf{Res}(a)(e_{00},e_{01},m_{0})=(f_{0}(e_{00}),f_{1}(e_{01}),m_{0}^{\prime}),
and

\mathsf{Res}(a)(\ell\odot(e_{00},e_{01},m_{0}))=\mathsf{Res}(a)(e_{00}^{\prime},e_{01},F_{00}(\ell)\odot m_{0})=(f_{0}(e_{00}^{\prime},f_{1}(e_{01}),[F_{00}(\ell)\odot m_{0}]^{\prime}))
.
We define the laxator mapping from the composite of the former two loose maps to the latter to be
(\mathrm{id}(f_{0}(e_{00}^{\prime})),\mathrm{id}(f_{1}(e_{01}),\lambda_{\ell,m_{0}}))
with
\lambda_{\ell,m_{0}}
the composite below:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{10}f_{0}e_{00}^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{10}f_{0}e_{00}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a_{0}F_{00}e_{00}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a_{1}F_{01}e_{01}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{11}f_{1}e_{01}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{10}f_{0}e_{00}^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a_{0}F_{00}e^{\prime}_{00}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a_{0}F_{00}e_{00}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a_{1}F_{01}e_{01}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{11}f_{1}e_{01}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{10}f_{0}e_{00}^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a_{0}F_{00}e^{\prime}_{00}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a_{1}F_{01}e_{01}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{11}f_{1}e_{01}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{10}f_{0}\ell}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\overline{f}_{0}e_{00})^{<}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{0}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\overline{f}_{0}\ell)^{<}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces am_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\overline{f}_{0}e_{00})^{>}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\overline{f}_{0}e^{\prime}_{00})^{<}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{0}F_{00}\ell}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces am_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\overline{f}_{0}e_{00})^{>}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\overline{f}_{0}e^{\prime}_{00})^{<}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces[F_{00}\ell\odot m_{0}]^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a(F_{00}\ell\odot m_{0})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\overline{f}_{0}e_{00})^{>}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{F_{00}\ell,m_{0}}}

Note here that
a_{\bullet,\bullet}
is the laxator of the double functor
a
.
Since
\overline{f}_{0}
is a
commuter transformation
, the globular cell
(\overline{f}_{0}\ell)^{<}
is a tight isomorphism.
Furthermore, since
a
is a pseudo-functor, this overall cell is a tight isomorphism.
This, and its correlate using
\overline{f}_{1}
, shows that
\mathsf{Res}(a)
will be a pseudo-functor of loose bimodules.
If only
a
were pseudo, then this double functor would still be lax on account of the square associated to
\overline{f}_{i}
;
this is why we must assume that the
\overline{f}_{i}
are commuter transformations in
\mathsf{Res}
.

∎

Action on 2-cells

Now suppose we have a 2-cell like so:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{E}_{0i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{E}_{1i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{E}_{00}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{E}_{1i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{E}_{0i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{E}_{1i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{=}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{M_{0i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{M_{1i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{M_{0i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{M_{1i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{M_{0i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{M_{1i}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{0i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{1i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{0i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{0i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{1i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{f}_{1i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{1i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{f}_{0i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{0i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{0i}}

We then define a tight transformation
\mathsf{Res}(\alpha,\cdots)
as follows:

1.

On
\mathbb{E}_{0i}
,
\mathsf{Res}(\alpha,\cdots)
is just
\phi_{i}
.

2.

The only nontrivial loose case to handle is
m_{0}:F_{00}e_{00}\mathrel{\mkern 3.0mu\vbox{\hbox{$\shortmid$}}\mkern-10.0mu{\to}}F_{01}e_{01}
, which we assign to the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{10}f_{00}e_{00}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(a_{00})F_{00}e_{00}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(a_{01})F_{01}e_{01}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{11}f_{01}e_{01}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{10}f_{10}e_{00}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(a_{10})F_{00}e_{00}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(a_{11})F_{01}e_{01}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{11}f_{11}e_{01}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\overline{f}_{00}e_{00})^{<}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{10}\phi_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{0}m_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{0}F_{00}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\overline{f}_{01}e_{00})^{>}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{1}F_{01}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{11}\phi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\overline{f}_{10}e_{00})^{<}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{1}m_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\overline{f}_{11}e_{00})^{>}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha m}

where the left and right squares are the transposes of the commutativity equation defining a 2-cell in
\mathcal{N}\mathsf{iche}
.

∎

Proposition 3.19

(Source and target of restricted loose bimodules)
.

The following diagram commutes.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{N}\mathsf{iche}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\ell}\mathcal{B}\mathsf{imod}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{2}\mathcal{C}\mathsf{at}^{\mathsf{colax}}(\Delta[1],\mathcal{D}\mathsf{bl})_{\mathsf{conj}}\times\mathsf{2}\mathcal{C}\mathsf{at}^{\mathsf{colax}}(\Delta[1],\mathcal{D}\mathsf{bl})_{\mathsf{comp}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{D}\mathsf{bl}\times\mathcal{D}\mathsf{bl}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Res}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(()_{0},()_{1})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{1}^{*}\times d_{1}^{*}}

On objects this means that given a niche,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{E}_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{E}_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{M_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{M_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}

its restriction is a loose bimodule whose source is
\mathbb{E}_{0}
and whose target is
\mathbb{E}_{1}
.

Proof.

This follows by definition of the 2-functor
\mathsf{Res}
given in
Theorem
3.18
.∎

As a corollary of
Theorem
3.18
, we conclude that restriction of a loose bimodule
\mathbb{M}\colon\mathbb{D}_{0}\mathrel{\mkern 3.0mu\vbox{\hbox{$\shortmid$}}\mkern-10.0mu{\to}}\mathbb{D}_{1}
along lax symmetric monoidal pseudo-double functors
F_{i}:\mathbb{E}_{i}\to\mathbb{D}_{i}
induces a symmetric monoidal structure on the restriction
\mathbb{M}(F_{0},F_{1})
so long as the unitors and laxitors of
F_{0}
(resp.
F_{1}
) are
conjoint (resp. companion) commuter transformations
. For the remainder of this section, we will expand on this observation.

3.3.2.1
Cartesian and symmetric monoidal structure in
\mathcal{N}\mathsf{iche}

Next we will investigate the cartesian and symmetric monoidal structure in
\mathcal{N}\mathsf{iche}
.

Lemma 3.20

(
\mathcal{N}\mathsf{iche}
, and
\mathcal{N}\mathsf{iche}^{\cong}
are cartesian 2-categories)
.

The
domain
\mathcal{N}\mathsf{iche}
(resp.
\mathcal{N}\mathsf{iche}^{\cong}
) of restriction of loose bimodules
is a cartesian 2-category, and its cartesian products may be constructed componentwise in
{\ell}\mathcal{B}\mathsf{imod}
and
\mathcal{D}\mathsf{bl}
.

In particular, the terminal object is the niche
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{L}\mathsf{oose}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{L}\mathsf{oose}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Hom}^{l}(\mathbb{L}\mathsf{oose})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}

including the endpoints of the
walking loose arrow
.

Proof.

As a pullback of cartesian functors between cartesian 2-categories,
\mathcal{N}\mathsf{iche}
is cartesian, and its products are constructed componentwise. This relies on knowing that
\mathsf{2}\mathcal{C}\mathsf{at}^{\mathsf{colax}}(\Delta[1],\mathcal{D}\mathsf{bl})_{\mathsf{comp}}
and
\mathsf{2}\mathcal{C}\mathsf{at}^{\mathsf{colax}}(\Delta[1],\mathcal{D}\mathsf{bl})_{\mathsf{conj}}
are cartesian, but they are since
\mathsf{2}\mathcal{C}\mathsf{at}^{\mathsf{colax}}(\Delta[1],\mathcal{D}\mathsf{bl})
is cartesian and a tight transformation into a product is a commuter if and only if its components are.

To see that
\mathcal{N}\mathsf{iche}^{\cong}
is also cartesian, it suffices to note that the double functor induced into a product or a pullback is pseudo when both of its components are.∎

We can use
symmetry of internalization
to characterize the symmetric monoidal and cartesian structure in
\mathcal{N}\mathsf{iche}
.

Lemma 3.21

(Symmetric monoidal objects of
\mathcal{N}\mathsf{iche}
and
\mathcal{N}\mathsf{iche}^{\cong}
)
.

A symmetric monoidal object of
\mathcal{N}\mathsf{iche}^{\cong}
(resp.
\mathcal{N}\mathsf{iche}
) is equivalently:

1.

A
symmetric monoidal loose bimodule

M:\mathbb{M}\to\mathbb{L}\mathsf{oose}
.

2.

Symmetric monoidal double categories
\mathbb{E}_{0}
and
\mathbb{E}_{1}
.

3.

Pseudo (resp. lax) symmetric monoidal pseudo-functors
F_{i}:\mathbb{E}_{i}\to M_{i}
, where the unitors and laxators of
F_{0}
are , and the unitors and laxators of
F_{1}
are .

Proof.

First, we note that the projection 2-functors
M:\mathcal{N}\mathsf{iche}\to{\ell}\mathcal{B}\mathsf{imod}
and
\mathbb{E}_{i}:\mathcal{N}\mathsf{iche}\to\mathcal{D}\mathsf{bl}_{u}
are cartesian, and therefore they preserve symmetric monoidal structure. It only remains to show that the colax squares
(\otimes_{i},\overline{\otimes}_{i}):F_{i}\times F_{i}\to F_{i}
and
(1_{i},\overline{1}_{i}):\{i\}\to F_{i}
assemble into a pseudo (resp. lax) monoidal morphism.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{E}_{i}\times\mathbb{E}_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{E}_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\{i\}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{E}_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{M_{i}\times M_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{M_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\{i\}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{M_{i}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\otimes_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{i}\times F_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\otimes}_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\otimes_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{1}_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1_{i}}

This follows by the
symmetry of internalization
(Theorem 7.4) of
[
ABK24
]
; specifically, a symmetric pseudo-monoid in maps with pseudo (resp. colax) squares is a pseudo (resp. lax) monoidal morphism.∎

Lemma 3.22

(Cartesian objects of
\mathcal{N}\mathsf{iche}^{\cong}
)
.

A cartesian object of
\mathcal{N}\mathsf{iche}^{\cong}
consists of

1.

A cartesian loose bimodule
M
.

2.

Cartesian double categories
\mathbb{E}_{0}
and
\mathbb{E}_{1}
.

3.

Cartesian pseudo functors
F_{i}:\mathbb{E}_{i}\to M_{i}
, such that the laxators and unitors of
F_{0}
are conjoints and of
F_{1}
are companions.

Proof.

We may again compute the cartesian objects componentwise in
{\ell}\mathcal{B}\mathsf{imod}
and
\mathsf{2}\mathcal{C}\mathsf{at}^{\mathsf{ps}}(\Delta[1],\mathcal{D}\mathsf{bl})^{2}
. By definition, a cartesian loose bimodule is a cartesian object in
{\ell}\mathcal{B}\mathsf{imod}
. On the other hand, by
symmetry of internalization
(Theorem 7.4 of
[
ABK24
]
), a cartesian object in
\mathsf{2}\mathcal{C}\mathsf{at}^{\mathsf{ps}}(\Delta[1],\mathcal{D}\mathsf{bl})
is equivalently a cartesian functor between cartesian double categories.∎

3.3.2.2
Restriction preserves cartesian and symmetric monoidal structure

Since restriction of loose bimodules is cartesian, it will preserve some 2-algebraic structure. However, since it is only a pseudo-functor, it won’t preserve all 2-algebraic structure. Specifically,
flexible
2-algebraic structure is preserved by cartesian pseudo-functors. Intuitively, 2-algebraic structure is
flexible
when it does not require any equations between 1-cells; this includes symmetric monoidal and cartesian structure. If 2-algebraic structure is flexible, then we can pass it through a pseudo-functor by conjugating every 2-cell by the unitors and laxators of the pseudo-functor.

As a corollary,
restriction of looes bimodules
\mathsf{Res}
will preserve symmetric monoidal and cartesian structure.

Recall that for a cartesian 2-category
\mathcal{K}
,

•

\mathcal{S}\mathsf{M}^{\mathsf{lax}}{(\mathcal{K})}
is the 2-category of symmetric monoidal objects in and lax monoidal morphisms.

•

\mathcal{C}\mathsf{art}(\mathcal{K})
is the 2-category of cartesian objects, cartesian maps, and general 2-cells.

Theorem 3.23

(Restriction of loose bimodules preserves symmetric monoidal and cartesian structure)
.

extends to pseudo-functors

\mathcal{S}\mathsf{M}(\mathcal{N}\mathsf{iche})\to\mathcal{S}\mathsf{M}({\ell}\mathcal{B}\mathsf{imod})

and

\mathcal{C}\mathsf{art}(\mathcal{N}\mathsf{iche})\to\mathcal{C}\mathsf{art}({\ell}\mathcal{B}\mathsf{imod}).

Similarly, for
\mathsf{Res}^{\cong}:\mathcal{N}\mathsf{iche}^{\cong}\to{\ell}\mathcal{B}\mathsf{imod}
.

Applying
this preservation of symmetric monoidal and cartesian structure
to the characterizations of
symmetric monoidal
and
cartesian
of
\mathcal{N}\mathsf{iche}
leads to the following conclusions:

•

Restriction of a symmetric monoidal loose bimodule by pseudo symmetric monoidal pseudo-functors (whose unitors and laxators are conjoints or companions, respectively) yields a symmetric monoidal loose bimodule.

•

Similarly, restriction of a symmetric monoidal loose bimodule by
lax
symmetric monoidal pseudo-functors (whose unitors and laxators are conjoint or companion
commuter transformations
, respectively) will yield a symmetric monoidal loose bimodule.

•

Finally, restriction of a cartesian bimodule by cartesian pseudofunctors yields a cartesian loose bimodule.

Explication 3.24

(Restriction of a symmetric monoidal loose bimodule)
.

Let

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{E}_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{E}_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{M_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{M_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}

be a
symmetric monoidal niche
.

By
Theorem
3.23
, the restricted loose bimodule
\mathbb{M}(F_{0},F_{1})
is a
symmetric monoidal loose bimodule
. Here we explicate the monoidal structure for loose heteromorphisms in
\mathbb{M}(F_{0},F_{1})
and show how it uses the conjoints of the laxator of
F_{0}
and companions of the laxator of
F_{1}
.

The laxator of
F_{0}
is depicted in the following diagram.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{E}_{0}\times\mathbb{E}_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{E}_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{M_{0}\times M_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{M_{0}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\otimes_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{0}\times F_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\otimes}_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\otimes_{0}}

It is a . So for the identity loose morphism on
F_{0}e_{0}\otimes F_{0}e_{0}^{\prime}
, we have the conjoint
\overline{\otimes}_{0}^{<}\colon F(e_{0}\otimes_{0}e_{0}^{\prime})\to Fe_{0}\otimes Fe_{0}^{\prime}
.

Likewise let
\overline{\otimes}_{1}
be the laxator of
F_{1}
. It is a companion commuter transformation. In particular, the companion of the laxator
\overline{\otimes}_{1}
applied to the identity loose morphism on
F_{1}e_{1}\otimes F_{1}e_{1}^{\prime}
induces a loose morphism
\overline{\phi}_{1}^{>}\colon F_{1}e_{1}\otimes F_{1}e_{1}^{\prime}\to F_{1}(e_{1}\otimes_{1}e_{1}^{\prime})
.

Now let
m\colon F_{0}e_{0}\mathrel{\mkern 3.0mu\vbox{\hbox{$\shortmid$}}\mkern-10.0mu{\to}}F_{1}e_{1}
and
m^{\prime}\colon F_{0}e_{0}^{\prime}\mathrel{\mkern 3.0mu\vbox{\hbox{$\shortmid$}}\mkern-10.0mu{\to}}F_{1}e_{1}^{\prime}
be . Then their monoidal product in the restriction
\mathbb{M}(F_{0},F_{1})
is defined to be the composite

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{0}(e_{0}\otimes_{0}e_{0}^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{0}e_{0}\otimes_{0}F_{0}e_{0}^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{1}e_{1}\otimes_{1}F_{1}e_{1}^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{1}(e_{1}\otimes_{1}e_{1}^{\prime})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\otimes_{0}}^{<}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m\otimes m^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\otimes_{1}}^{>}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}

This formula works in all the cases above; but if the
F_{i}
are pseudo-symmetric monoidal, then the
\overline{\otimes}_{i}
will be isomorphisms, and if everything involved is cartesian, then these will furthermore be cartesian products.

Remark 3.25

(Remark on the preservation of cartesian structure by certain colax 2-functors)
.

As remarked in the , we could actually end up with a
normal colax
2-functor
\mathsf{Res}^{\mathsf{lax}}:\mathcal{N}\mathsf{iche}^{\mathsf{lax}}\to{\ell}\mathcal{B}\mathsf{imod}^{\mathsf{lax}}
if we loosened up the definition of
\mathcal{N}\mathsf{iche}
. This normal colax 2-functor would continue to preserve products, and moreover it would have the special property that the colaxator associated to composition with a product projection, from either side, is an isomorphism.

This suggests a general consideration concering colax
\mathcal{F}
-functors between
\mathcal{F}
-categories. It appears that the correct notion of such a functor is to be a colax 2-functor, but where the counitor is an isomorphism (it is normal) and the colaxator associated to composition with an inert morphism, on either side, is an isomorphism. We may see normality as a nullary case of the latter condition.

A bit of fiddling has made it seem reasonable to expect that such colax
\mathcal{F}
-functors which preserve products would also preserve cartesian objects (this can be straightforwardly verified) and furthermore induce a push-forward on the
\mathcal{F}
-categories of cartesian objects (this would benefit from a theoretical explanation). We leave it for an intrepid 2-algebraist to continue these musings.

We finish this remark by noting that a theorem of the above sort would give us a slight advantage in the double operadic theory of systems: we would know that the restriction of pre-cartesian loose bimodules by pre-cartesian pseudo-functors is still pre-cartesian. This is only a slight improvement on the results of this section; nevertheless, the general question is interesting on its own.

Explication 3.26

(Summary of results of
Section
3.3.2
)
.

We may put together the calculations
Lemma
3.21
and
Lemma
3.22
to conclude the following:

1.

Restriction of a symmetric monoidal loose bimodule by pseudo symmetric monoidal pseudo-functors (whose unitors and laxators are conjoints or companions, respectively) yields a symmetric monoidal loose bimodule.

2.

Similarly, restriction of a symmetric monoidal loose bimodule by
lax
symmetric monoidal pseudo-functors (whose unitors and laxators are conjoint or companion
commuter transformations
, respectively) will yield a symmetric monoidal loose bimodule.

3.

Finally, restriction of a cartesian bimodule by cartesian pseudofunctors yields a cartesian loose bimodule.

We will use these constructions liberally to produce examples of systems theories as symmetric monoidal loose right modules.

Explicitly, if
m_{0}\in\mathbb{M}(F_{0}e_{00},F_{1}e_{10})
and
m_{1}\in\mathbb{M}(F_{0}e_{01},F_{1}e_{11})
are loose heteromorphisms of the restricted loose bimodule, then their monoidal product
m_{0}\otimes_{\mathsf{res}}m_{1}
in the restriction is defined to be

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{0}(e_{00}\otimes_{0}e_{01})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{0}e_{00}\otimes_{0}F_{0}e_{01}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{1}e_{10}\otimes_{1}F_{1}e_{11}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{1}(e_{10}\otimes_{1}e_{11})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\otimes_{0}}^{<}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{0}\otimes m_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\otimes_{1}}^{>}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}

where
\overline{\otimes}_{i}
are the laxators of
\otimes_{i}:\mathbb{E}_{i}\times\mathbb{E}_{i}\to\mathbb{E}_{i}
, and we’ve taken the conjoint and companion respectively. This formula works in all the cases above; but if the
F_{i}
are pseudo-symmetric monoidal, then the
\overline{\otimes}_{i}
will be isomorphisms, and if everything involved is cartesian, then these will furthermore be cartesian products.

4
Symmetric monoidal loose right modules as modules of systems

In this section, we define symmetric monoidal loose right modules and describe how they appropriately organize a collection of systems, their interactions, and their maps. In other words, we will show how the answers to the questions posed in
Informal definition
1.1
form a symmetric monoidal loose right module. To emphasize this attitude we will often refer to a symmetric monoidal loose right module as a
module of systems over a double category of interactions
. We also give several examples of this attitude that we will make formal in
Section
8.3
.

4.1
Module of systems over interactions

We are now ready to give the main attitude of this section: that of a
module of systems over a double category of interactions
. We begin by definining symmetric monoidal loose right modules as symmetric monoidal objects of the
2-category of loose right modules
.

Definition 4.1

(The 2-category of symmetric monoidal loose right modules)
.

The 2-category
\mathcal{S}\mathsf{M}({\ell}\mathcal{M}\mathsf{od}_{\mathsf{r}})
is the 2-category of symmetric pseudo-monoids and pseudo-morphisms in the cartesian 2-category
{\ell}\mathcal{M}\mathsf{od}_{\mathsf{r}}
of
loose right modules
. We call the objects of this 2-category
symmetric monoidal loose right modules
.

It is helpful to view a symmetric monoidal loose right module as a symmetric monoidal category with an action.

Explication 4.2

(Symmetric monoidal loose right module)
.

We can think of a symmetric monoidal loose right module
\mathbb{M}\colon\bullet\mathrel{\mkern 3.0mu\vbox{\hbox{$\shortmid$}}\mkern-10.0mu{\to}}\mathbb{D}_{1}
as consisting of:

•

Its target, a symmetric monoidal double category
\mathbb{D}_{1}
.

•

Its carrier, a symmetric monoidal category
\mathsf{Car}(\mathbb{M})
.

•

A functor from the carrier to the tight category of the target,
\mathsf{Car}(\mathbb{M})\to\mathsf{Tight}({\mathbb{D}_{1}})
.

•

An action of the loose category of the target on the carrier
\mathsf{Loose}({\mathbb{D}_{1}})\curvearrowright\mathsf{Car}(\mathbb{M})
.

By symmetry of internalization, a symmetric monoidal loose right module is a right pseudo-module internal to symmetric monoidal categories.

We now interpret a
symmetric monoidal loose right module
as an
module of systems
over a symmetric monoidal double category of
interfaces and interactions
.

Attitude 4.3

(Module of systems)
.

We will often consider a
symmetric monoidal loose right module
to organize the data of systems, their interactions, and their maps. When we do so, we will refer to this module as a
module of systems
.

Notation 4.4

(Module of systems)
.

Given a
module of systems

\mathbb{S}\colon\bullet\mathrel{\mkern 3.0mu\vbox{\hbox{$\shortmid$}}\mkern-10.0mu{\to}}\mathbb{I}
, we say that:

•

The
target

\mathbb{I}
is the symmetric monoidal double category of
interfaces
and
interactions
mediating them.

•

The
carrier

\mathsf{Car}(\mathbb{S})
is the symmetric monoidal category of
systems
and
system maps
.

Following the phrase a "module over a ring", we often call
\mathbb{S}
a
module of systems over interactions,
\mathbb{I}
.

To further make sense of how a module of systems organizes the data of systems, their interactions, and their maps, it is helpful to rename the objects, morphisms, and squares of
\mathbb{L}\mathsf{oose}
so that the names give attitudes to each objects, morphisms, and squares that they label. These attitudes were introduced in
ontology of double categorical systems theory
.

Attitude 4.5

(Reinterpretting the walking loose arrow for modules of systems)
.

Let
\mathbb{S}\to\mathbb{L}\mathsf{oose}
be a
module of systems
. This functor labels the objects, morphisms, and squares of
\mathbb{S}
by the objects, morphisms, and squares of
\mathbb{L}\mathsf{oose}
. When considering
\mathbb{S}
as a
module of systems
, it is helpful to rename the objects, morphisms, and squares of
\mathbb{L}\mathsf{oose}
so that they assign meaningful labels to the different components of the module of systems,
\mathbb{S}
.

First, recall the components of
\mathbb{L}\mathsf{oose}
from
Explication
3.2
.

Explication 4.6
.

The
walking loose arrow double category

\mathbb{L}\mathsf{oose}
has two objects, three loose arrows (two identities along with the walking loose arrow
2_{\ell}
), two identity tight arrows, and three identity squares. These are all pictured below.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}

Next we strategically name each component.

•

The source
of a module of systems is the symmetric monoidal double category living over the left object
0
and its identity morphisms and square. Since the source of a systems theory is the terminal double category, we will rename the left object
\bullet
.

•

The target
of a module of systems is the symmetric monoidal double category living over the right object
1
and its identity morphisms and square. We adopt the attitude that it represents
interfaces
and
interactions
that relate them. Therefore, we name the right object
\mathsf{interface}
, its tight identity
\mathsf{interface\,map}
, its loose identity
\mathsf{interaction}
, and its identity square
\mathsf{interaction\,map}
.

•

The carrier
of a module of systems is the symmetric monoidal category living over the walking arrow and its identity square. We adopt the attitude that the loose morphisms in module of systems labeled by the walking arrow will represent
systems
while the squares labeled by the identity square for the walking arrow represent
system maps
. Therefore, we name the walking loose arrow
\mathsf{system}
and its identity square
\mathsf{system\,map}
.

Collectively, these renamings result in the following new presentation of the walking loose arrow
\mathbb{L}\mathsf{oose}
. Note that in this presentation we no longer show the identity morphisms and square for the left object
\bullet
, because in a systems theory they do not label any non-identity morphisms and squares.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{interface}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{interface}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{interface}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{interface}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{system}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{interaction}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{interface\,map}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{system}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{interaction}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{system\,map}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{interaction\,map}}

Figure 3:
Ontology of double categorical systems theory

The labels and their interactions are a scheme for interpretting a module of systems.

Explication 4.7

(Labelling perspective for modules of systems)
.

Given a module of systems
\mathbb{S}\to\mathbb{L}\mathsf{oose}
, we refer to the objects living over
\mathsf{interface}
as
interfaces
, the loose morphisms living over
\mathsf{system}
as
systems
, the loose morphisms living over
\mathsf{interaction}
as
interactions
, and so forth for the tight morphisms and squares.

To elaborate the notation in
Notation
4.4
, this means that given a module of systems
\mathbb{S}\colon\bullet\mathrel{\mkern 3.0mu\vbox{\hbox{$\shortmid$}}\mkern-10.0mu{\to}}\mathbb{I}
.

•

The target category
\mathbb{I}
is the symmetric monoidal double category whose:

–

Objects are interfaces.

–

Tight morphisms are interface maps.

–

Loose morphisms are interactions.

–

Squares are interaction maps.

•

The carrier
\mathsf{Car}(\mathbb{S})
is the symmetric monoidal category whose:

–

Objects are systems.

–

Morphisms are system maps.

We can derive features of a systems theory from the structure of
\mathbb{L}\mathsf{oose}
. For example,

•

Every system has an interface (its codomain).

•

The composite of a system and an interaction is again a system. This composition is what we mean by
interactions acting on systems
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{interface}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{interface}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{system}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{system}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{interaction}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}

Finally, the symmetric monoidal structure implies that systems, interactions, and their maps can be composed in parallel. This feature is critical for the operadic perspective in which an interaction may act on several independent, component systems, whose result is a single composite system.

We can now re-interpret
the explication of a symmetric monoidal loose right module
from the perspective of a module of systems.

Explication 4.8

(Module of systems)
.

A module of systems
\mathbb{S}\colon\bullet\mathrel{\mkern 3.0mu\vbox{\hbox{$\shortmid$}}\mkern-10.0mu{\to}}\mathbb{I}
consists of:

•

A symmetric monoidal double category of interactions
\mathbb{I}
.

•

A symmetric monoidal category of systems
\mathsf{Car}(\mathsf{S})
.

•

A functor assigning systems to their interface,
\mathsf{Car}(\mathsf{S})\to\mathsf{Tight}({\mathbb{I}})
.

•

An action of interactions on systems
\mathsf{Loose}({\mathbb{I}})\curvearrowright\mathsf{Car}(\mathsf{S})
that respects the interface.

Remark 4.9

(Restriction of modules of systems)
.

If
\mathbb{S}:\bullet\mathrel{\mkern 3.0mu\vbox{\hbox{$\shortmid$}}\mkern-10.0mu{\to}}\mathbb{I}
is a
module of systems
and
r:\mathbb{J}\to\mathbb{I}
is a lax symmetric monoidal double functor (with unitors and laxators
\mu

companion commuters
) which we think of as giving a restriction of the double category of interactions from
\mathbb{I}
to
\mathbb{J}
, then for any two systems
S_{1}:\bullet\mathrel{\mkern 3.0mu\vbox{\hbox{$\shortmid$}}\mkern-10.0mu{\to}}rJ_{1}
and
S_{2}:\bullet\mathrel{\mkern 3.0mu\vbox{\hbox{$\shortmid$}}\mkern-10.0mu{\to}}rJ_{2}
are systems, their parallel product as systems over the interface double category
\mathbb{J}
is the loose composite:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{rJ_{1}\otimes rJ_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{r(J_{1}\otimes J_{2})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{1}\otimes S_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{1}\otimes_{r}S_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu^{>}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}

4.2
Examples of modules of systems

In this section, we will give illustrative examples of modules of systems that are constructed via the doctrines that will be presented in
Section
6
and
Section
7
. We give their full definition in
Section
8.3
but introduce them here to exemplify how modules of systems answer the questions posed in
Informal definition
1.1
. Additionally, these examples either generalize or restructure categorical systems theories presented in the literature.

4.2.1
Module of open Petri nets over undirected wiring diagrams

Here we’ll give an example of a module of systems

\mathbb{C}\mathsf{ospan}(\mathsf{Petri})(\emptyset,\mathbb{C}\mathsf{ospan}(\mathsf{Finset}))\colon\bullet\mathrel{\mkern 3.0mu\vbox{\hbox{$\shortmid$}}\mkern-10.0mu{\to}}\mathbb{C}\mathsf{ospan}(\mathsf{Finset})

by which Petri nets compose via undirected wiring diagrams. This example gives an operadic perspective on the symmetric monoidal double category of open Petri nets introduced in
[
BCV22a
]
. This systems theory is generated using the restriction of
the port plugging doctrine
to
a free process
.

We begin by defining Petri nets.

4.2.1.1
Petri nets

Definition 4.10

(Petri net)
.

A Petri net consists of a finite set of species
S
, a finite set of transitions
T
and two functions
s,t\colon T\to\mathbb{N}[S]
assigning to each transition a source multiset of species and a target multiset of species.

Example 4.11

(Petri net)
.

The following Petri net has two species
S
and
I
which represent a susceptible and infected population. The single transition represents an infection event whose source is
(S,I)
and whose target is
(I,I)
. In other words, an infection takes a susceptible and an infected individual and transforms them into two infected individuals.

The double categorical perspective taken in
[
BCV22a
]
extends the single categorical perspective introduced in
[
BP17
]
by introducing the concept of
maps between Petri nets
.

Definition 4.12

(Maps of Petri nets)
.

Given Petri nets

P=(S,T,s\colon T\to\mathbb{N}[S],t\colon T\to\mathbb{N}[S])

and

P^{\prime}=(S^{\prime},T^{\prime},s^{\prime}\colon T^{\prime}\to\mathbb{N}[S^{\prime}],t^{\prime}\colon T^{\prime}\to\mathbb{N}[S^{\prime}]),

a
map of Petri nets

P\to P^{\prime}
consists of maps
f\colon S\to S^{\prime}
and
g\colon T\to T^{\prime}
satisfying

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{N}[S]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{N}[S]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{N}[S^{\prime}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{N}[S^{\prime}]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{N}[f]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{N}[f]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t^{\prime}}

Example 4.13

(Maps of Petri nets)
.

Below depicts a map from a Petri net representing an SIR model of infection to a Petri net representing an SIS model of infection, in which recovered patients are susceptible to the disease once again.

Colors outlining the species and transitions indicate the maps between species and transitions. In particular, the susceptible and recovered populations of the domain Petri net are mapped to the susceptible population of the codomain Petri net. The infected population of the domain Petri net is mapped to the intefected population of the codomain Petri net.

Petri nets and their maps form a category.

Definition 4.14

(Category of Petri nets)
.

Let
\mathsf{Petri}
be the category whose objects are
Petri nets
and whose morphisms are
maps of Petri nets
.

From
[
BM20
]
this category has small colimits and hence is
rex
.

4.2.1.2
The double category of interfaces and interactions in the module of open Petri nets

Recall
the target of a module of systems
is a symmetric monoidal double category whose tight category consists of interfaces and interface maps and whose loose category consists of interactions and interaction maps. For the module of open Petri nets, the double category of interfaces and interactions is the double category of cospans of finite sets,
\mathbb{C}\mathsf{ospan}(\mathsf{Finset})
, which we define below.

Definition 4.15

(The double category
\mathbb{C}\mathsf{ospan}(\mathsf{Finset})
)
.

The double category
\mathbb{C}\mathsf{ospan}(\mathsf{Finset})
has:

•

Objects are finite sets,
M
.

•

A tight morphism is a map of finite sets,
f\colon M\to M^{\prime}
.

•

A loose morphism is a cospan of finite sets,
M\to J\leftarrow N
.

•

A square is a commuting diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{M^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{J^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{N^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n}

Next we highlight how we depict the components of
\mathbb{C}\mathsf{ospan}(\mathsf{Finset})
as interfaces, interactions, and their maps labelled by the objects and morphisms of
\mathbb{L}\mathsf{oose}
.

Example 4.16

(Interfaces in the module of open Petri nets)
.

In the module of open Petri nets, interfaces and their maps are the tight category of
\mathbb{C}\mathsf{ospan}(\mathsf{Finset})
. Hence, an interface is a finite set and an interface map is a map of finite sets.

We depict an interface
M
as a box with
M
exposed ports. For example, below is the interface
2
.

Note that our depiction shows how
M
is an object of
\mathbb{C}\mathsf{ospan}(\mathsf{Petri})(\emptyset,\mathbb{C}\mathsf{ospan}(\mathsf{Finset}))
living over the object
\mathsf{interface}
in
\mathbb{L}\mathsf{oose}
.

The following diagram depicts an interface map
3\to 2
. Colors indicate that the first and third ports (resp. second port) of the domain interface are sent to the first port (resp. second port) of the codomain interface.

Example 4.17

(Interactions in the module of open Petri nets)
.

In the module of open Petri nets, interactions and their maps are the loose category of
\mathbb{C}\mathsf{ospan}(\mathsf{Finset})
. Hence an interaction is given by a cospan of finite sets and a map of interactions is a map of cospans.

For example, an interaction that transforms two systems each with interface
2
into a single system with interface
3
is a cospan
2+2\to J\leftarrow 3
. The following cospan is an example of such an interaction.
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{2+2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 3}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 3}

We depict this interaction as the following
undirected wiring diagram
living over the loose identity
\mathsf{interaction}\colon\mathsf{interface}\to\mathsf{interface}
.

Let
m\colon M\to M^{\prime}
and
n\colon N\to N^{\prime}
be two
maps of interfaces
and let
M\to J\leftarrow N
and
M^{\prime}\to J^{\prime}\leftarrow N^{\prime}
be two interactions. Recall, that an
interaction map
in the module of open Petri nets is a map
J\to J^{\prime}
such that the following diagram commutes.
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{M^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{J^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{N^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n}

Below is an example of an interaction map that is the inclusion of an undirected wiring diagram with 3 junctions into an undirected wiring diagram with 4 junctions.

It lives over the following identity square in
\mathbb{L}\mathsf{oose}
.
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{interface}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{interface}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{interface}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{interface}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{interaction}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{interface\,map}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{interface\,map}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{interaction}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{interaction\,map}}

4.2.1.3
System and system maps in the module of open Petri nets

Recall that the carrier of a module of systems is a symmetric monoidal category whose objects are systems and whose morphisms are system maps. In this section, we describe the carrier of the module of open Petri nets.

Example 4.18

(Systems in the module of open Petri nets)
.

In the module of open Petri nets, a
system
is an
open Petri net
, which we define below.

Definition 4.19

(Open Petri net)
.

An
open Petri net
consists of:

•

A finite set
M
representing its interface.

•

A Petri net
(S,T,s\colon T\to\mathbb{N}[S],t\colon T\to\mathbb{N}[S])
.

•

A map
p\colon M\to S
which determines the species that each port exposes.

In the module of open Petri nets, an open Petri net is a loose morphism whose codomain is its interface and which lives over the walking loose arrow
\mathsf{system}\colon\bullet\mathrel{\mkern 3.0mu\vbox{\hbox{$\shortmid$}}\mkern-10.0mu{\to}}\mathsf{interface}
.

Below is an example of an open Petri net with interface
2
.
This Petri net models an infection event
. The first port exposes the susceptiple population
S
and the second port exposes the infected population
I
.

Here is a second example of an open Petri net with interface
2
. This Petri net models a recovery event. The first port exposes the infected population
I
and the second port exposes the recovered population
R
.

Example 4.20

(Monoidal product for open Petri nets)
.

The module of open Petri nets is equipped with a symmetric monoidal structure that we can use to "stack" systems and interfaces.

For example, below is the monoidal product of the two open Petri nets representing infection and recovery events. The interface for this monoidal product system is the monoidal product of the interfaces, which in this example is given by coproduct:
2+2
.

Example 4.21

(System maps for open Petri nets)
.

In the module of open Petri nets , a
system map
is a map of open Petri nets, which we define below.

Definition 4.22

(Maps of open Petri nets)
.

A map from an open Petri net with interface
M

(P=(S,T,s\colon T\to\mathbb{N}[S],t\colon T\to\mathbb{N}[S]),p\colon M\to S)

to an open Petri net with interface
M^{\prime}

(P^{\prime}=(S^{\prime},T^{\prime},s^{\prime}\colon T^{\prime}\to\mathbb{N}[S^{\prime}],t^{\prime}\colon T^{\prime}\to\mathbb{N}[S^{\prime}]),p^{\prime}\colon M^{\prime}\to S),

along an interface map

m\colon M\to M^{\prime}

consists of a
map of Petri nets

(f\colon S\to S^{\prime},g\colon T\to T^{\prime})\colon P\to P^{\prime}
such that the following diagram commutes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{M^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{S^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{\prime}}

Note that a system map is
between
two
systems
and
along
a
map between the interfaces
of those systems. Furthermore, it is labelled by the identity square (below called
\mathsf{system\,map}
) for the walking loose arrow (below called
\mathsf{system}
) in
\mathbb{L}\mathsf{oose}
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{interface}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{interface}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{system}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{interface\,map}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{system}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{system\,map}}

Below is an example of a system map between an open SIR Petri net and an open SIS Petri net along the interface map exemplified
here
.

This type of system map has the quality of
abstraction
because it abstracts away the distinction between the susceptible and recovered populations while retaining the overal dynamics of the system.

Next we have an example of a system map from an open SIR Petri net to a open SIRD Petri net — in which infected patients either recover or die — along the indicated map of interfaces.

This type of system map has the quality of
embedding
because it shows how the dynamics of the original SIR model are embedded in a larger model.

Example 4.23

(The action of interactions on systems in the module of open Petri nets)
.

Finally, we show how interactions (and their map) act on systems (and their maps) in the module of open Petri nets.

Example 4.24

(The action of undirected wiring diagrams on open Petri nets)
.

Recall that
\mathsf{interaction}
is the loose identity on
\mathsf{interface}
. Therefore, its composite with the walking loose arrow

\mathsf{system}\colon\bullet\mathrel{\mkern 3.0mu\vbox{\hbox{$\shortmid$}}\mkern-10.0mu{\to}}\mathsf{interface}

is again the walking loose arrow
\mathsf{system}
. In other words, the diagram below commutes.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{interface}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{interface}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{system}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{system}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{interaction}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}

We intrepret this fact as "an interaction transforms one system into another system".

Consider the following composite in the module of open Petri nets.

The undirected wiring diagram living over
\mathsf{interaction}
transforms the two systems representing infection and recovery events living over
\mathsf{system}
. As we will see mathematically in a future section, it identifies the infected species exposed by the two open Petri nets.

The composite of this process with these systems is again a system: an open Petri net representing an SIR infection model.

Example 4.25

(Maps of composites)
.

We saw how
interactions act on systems
follows from the fact that in
\mathbb{L}\mathsf{oose}
the composite

\bullet\xrightarrow{\mathsf{system}}\mathsf{interface}\xrightarrow{\mathsf{interaction}}\mathsf{interface}

is the map
\bullet\xrightarrow{\mathsf{system}}\mathsf{interface}
.

Likewise, maps of interactions act on maps of systems because in
\mathbb{L}\mathsf{oose}
the composite of the squares

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{interface}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{interface}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{interface}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{interface}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{system}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{interaction}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{system}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{interaction}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{system\,map}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{interaction\,map}}

is the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{interface}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{interface}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{system}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{system}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{system\,map}}

Below is an example of a map of systems (left) acted on by a map of processes (right). For simplicity we have omitted the colors indicating the details of these maps and leave them to be inferred by the reader.

4.2.2
Module of Moore machines over lenses

Here we’ll give an example of a module of systems

\mathbb{M}\mathsf{oore}(\mathsf{proj}_{\mathsf{Set}},(-)^{2})\colon\bullet\mathrel{\mkern 3.0mu\vbox{\hbox{$\shortmid$}}\mkern-10.0mu{\to}}\mathbb{L}\mathsf{ens}(\mathsf{proj}_{\mathsf{Set}})

by which deterministic Moore machines composing via lenses. For more on Moore machines, the monograph
[
Mye21
]
goes into great detail about these sorts of systems and their behaviors in its first few chapter. In particular, the kinds of Moore machines we will cover here (the traditional ones) are covered in Chapter 1 of
[
Mye21
]
, their non-deterministic variants are covered in Chapter 2, and their maps and behaviors are covered in Chapter 3. In this paper, we will see Moore machines and their variants again in the guise of
generalized Moore machines
.

The construction of this module of systems is defined in
Section
7.3
. In this section we exemplify its systems, interactions, and their maps.

4.2.2.1
The double category of interfaces and interactions in the module of deterministic Moore machines

Explication 4.26

(The double category
\mathbb{L}\mathsf{ens}(\mathsf{proj}_{\mathsf{Set}})
)
.

The double category of interactions for the module of deterministic Moore machines is the
lense double category

\mathbb{L}\mathsf{ens}(\mathsf{proj}_{\mathsf{Set}})
where
\mathsf{proj}_{\mathsf{Set}}
is the
simple fibration
associated with
\mathsf{Set}
, which has:

•

An object is a pair of sets which we denote
{A^{\#}\choose A}
.

•

A loose morphism is a pair of set maps
f\colon A\to B
and
f^{\#}\colon A\times B^{\#}\to A^{\#}
which we notate as a lens
{f^{\#}\choose f}\colon{A^{\sharp}\choose A}\leftrightarrows{B^{\sharp}\choose B}
. See also
Example
2.38
.

•

A tight morphism is a pair of set maps
f\colon A\to B
and
f^{\#}\colon A^{\#}\times B\to B^{\#}
which we notate as a chart
{f^{\#}\choose f}\colon{A^{\sharp}\choose A}\rightrightarrows{B^{\sharp}\choose B}
.

Example 4.27

(Interactions in the module of deterministic Moore machines)
.

Since interactions are lenses interaction that transforms a
Moore machine
with interface
{I\choose O}
into a Moore machine with interface
{I^{\prime}\choose O^{\prime}}
consists of:

•

A map of outputs
f\colon O\to O^{\prime}
.

•

A map of inputs in the reverse order
f^{\#}\colon I^{\prime}\times O\to I
.

Let
2
be the two element set
\{0,1\}
. There is an interaction

{2\choose 2}\times{2\choose 2}\mathrel{\mkern 3.0mu\vbox{\hbox{$\shortmid$}}\mkern-10.0mu{\to}}{2\choose 2\times 2}

where

•

The map on outputs is the swap
(b_{1},b_{2})\mapsto(b_{2},b_{1})
.

•

The map of inputs is given by
(a,(b_{1},b_{2}))\mapsto(a,b_{1})
.

This interaction is depicted by the directed wiring diagram below where each wire carries a copy of
2
.

This interaction transforms two Moore machines (called the components) with interface
{2\choose 2}
into a single Moore machine (called the composite) with interface
{2\choose 2\times 2}
.

•

The composite machine outputs the pair of outputs generated by the components.

•

Given an input to the composite machine, that input is sent directly to the first machine and the output of the first component machine is used as the input to the second component machine.

We will show how this interaction acts on systems in
Example
4.31
.

4.2.2.2
Systems and the action of interactions in the module of deterministic Moore machines

Definition 4.28

(Open deterministic Moore machines)
.

A deterministic Moore machine with interface
{I\choose O}
consists of:

•

A set of states
S
.

•

An update
u\colon I\times S\to S
.

•

A readout
r\colon S\to O
.

Note that a deterministic Moore machine is equivalently a
lens

{u\choose r}\colon{S\choose S}\leftrightarrows{I\choose O}

Example 4.29

(Mod 2 counter)
.

Below is a
deterministic Moore machine
with interface
{2\choose 2}
. It has two states, its update function counts modulo 2, and its readout is the identity.

Example 4.30

(Mod 4 counter)
.

Below is a
deterministic Moore machine
with interface
{2\choose 2}
. It has 4 states, its update function counts modulo 4, and its readout is the parity of the state.

Example 4.31

(The action of lenses on deterministic Moore machines)
.

Consider composing two
mod 2 counters
according to the interaction described in
Example
4.27
. The result is a mod 4 counter.

A state of the composite system is the product of the states of the component systems. The first component system is tracking the
1
s digit in the binary representation of a number and the second component system is tracking the
2
s digit.

So far we have seen two examples of Moore machines:
the mod 2 counter
and
the mod 4 counter
. In both of these examples, the read out is an isomorphism, which means that the whole state is exposed by the interface.

Consider composing
the mod 4 counter
with an interaction that
{2\choose 2\times 2}\mathrel{\mkern 3.0mu\vbox{\hbox{$\shortmid$}}\mkern-10.0mu{\to}}{2\choose 2}
that forgets the first output. The result is a mod 4 counter whose
output exposes only the parity of each state
. The states
00
and
10
output
0
while the states
01
and
11
output
1
.

4.2.2.3
System maps in the module of deterministic Moore machines

In this section, we will show how maps of Moore machines can represent trajectories.

Example 4.32

(Trajectories in deterministic Moore machine)
.

A trajectory in a Moore machine is a sequence of inputs and a sequence of states that evolves according to the sequence of inputs. For example, in the
mod 2 counter
, one example trajectory consists of the input sequence

\cdots\;1\;1\;1\;1\;1\;1\;\cdots

and the state sequence

\cdots\;0\;1\;0\;1\;0\;\cdots

because if the mod 2 counter receives a sequence of input it will cycle through the four states.

Another example of a trajectory consists of the input sequence

\cdots\;0\;0\;0\;0\;0\;\cdots

and the state sequence

\cdots\;0\;0\;0\;0\;0\;\cdots

because if the system starts in state
0
and receives only
0
inputs, then it will remain in state
0
. Note that the input sequence

\cdots\;0\;0\;0\;0\;0\;\cdots

and the state sequence

\cdots\;1\;1\;1\;1\;1\;\cdots.

This example shows that the input sequence alone does not uniquely determine a trajectory.

In the remainder of this section we will show how a trajectory in a Moore machine is represented by system maps of a certain sort.

Example 4.33

(The timeline Moore machine)
.

Consider the
Moore machine
below. Its interface is
{1\choose\mathbb{Z}}
and states
\mathbb{Z}
. The single input increments the state and the readout outputs the entire state.

This Moore machine functions like a timeline. Each state is a time and the input incremements the clock.

Example 4.34

(Interface maps of Moore machines)
.

A map of interfaces
{I_{1}\choose O_{1}}\to{I_{2}\choose O_{2}}
consists of

•

A map of outputs
O_{1}\to O_{2}
.

•

A map of inputs
I_{1}\times O_{1}\to I_{2}
.

Given a Moore machine with interface
{I\choose O}
. We can index the trajectories by the behavior of the trajectory on its interface. The behavior of a trajectory on its interface is a sequence of inputs and outputs. This behavior is captured by a map of interface
{1\choose\mathbb{Z}}\to{I\choose O}
.

For example, below is a map of interfaces
{1\choose\mathbb{Z}}\to{2\choose 2}
consisting of the input sequence of all
1
s and output sequence that alternates between
0
and
1
. This map defines a behavior on the interface
{2\choose 2}
that is: output alternating
0
s and
1
s when receiving the input
1
.

Example 4.35

(Maps of Moore machines)
.

A map of Moore machines along a given
interface map
consists of a map of states such that at each state the behavior of the systems on their interfaces are compatible according to the correspondence defined by the interface map.

Explicitly, for
i=1,2
suppose we have
Moore machines

(S_{i},u_{i}\colon I_{i}\times S_{i}\to S_{i},r_{i}\colon S_{i}\to O_{i})

with interfaces
{I_{i}\choose O_{i}}
. A
map of Moore machines
along
the map of interfaces

(h\colon O_{1}\to O_{2},h_{\#}\colon I_{1}\times O_{1}\to I_{2})

consists of a map of states
s\colon S_{1}\to S_{2}
satisfying:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{S_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{O_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{I_{1}\times S_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{S_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{S_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{O_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{I_{1}\times O_{1}\times S_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{I_{2}\times S_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{S_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I_{1}\times(r_{1},s)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{\#}\times S_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{2}}

Below is an example of a system map from the
timeline Moore machine
to
the mod 2 counter
along
the interface map
depicted above. The map of states defines a sequence of states (in other words, a trajectory) that produces the desired behavior on the interface.

In this example the map of states was uniquely defined by the interface map. However, the next example shows that this is not always the case.

Using
the same interface map
, we can instead define a trajectory of the
mod 4 counter that outputs only the parity of the state
as a system map out of
the timeline Moore machine
. There are two such system maps because states
00
and state
10
produce the same behavior on the interface.

5
Constructing modules of systems via doctrines

In this section, we will introduce the notion of a
doctrine of systems theories
which is a formula for constructing particular classes of modules of systems from more primitive data than the module itself. In the following sections we will give examples of doctrines which reconstruct modules of systems that are prevalent in the literature.

5.1
Doctrines of systems theories

We begin by giving a definition of a doctrines of systems theory. This definition is intentionally lightweight.

Definition 5.1

(Doctrine of systems theories)
.

A
doctrine of systems theories
consists of a
cartesian 2-functor

\pi_{\mathcal{D}\mathsf{}}:\mathcal{D}\mathsf{}_{\mathsf{sys}}\to\mathcal{D}\mathsf{}_{\mathsf{inter}}
together with a (strictly) commuting square of cartesian pseudo-functors
