{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{D}\mathsf{}_{\mathsf{sys}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\ell}\mathcal{M}\mathsf{od}_{\mathsf{r}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{D}\mathsf{}_{\mathsf{inter}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{D}\mathsf{bl}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{S}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\mathcal{D}\mathsf{}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(-)_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{I}}

Figure 4:
Doctrine of systems theories

into the
2-categories of loose right modules
and double categories.

We often notate such a doctrine by the triple,

(\pi_{\mathcal{D}\mathsf{}}\colon\mathcal{D}\mathsf{}_{\mathsf{sys}}\to\mathcal{D}\mathsf{}_{\mathsf{inter}},\mathbb{S},\mathbb{I}).

A doctrine induces pseudofunctors

\mathcal{S}\mathsf{M}(\mathcal{D}\mathsf{}_{\mathsf{sys}})\to\mathcal{S}\mathsf{M}({\ell}\mathcal{M}\mathsf{od}_{\mathsf{r}})

and

\mathcal{C}\mathsf{art}(\mathcal{D}\mathsf{}_{\mathsf{sys}})\to\mathcal{C}\mathsf{art}({\ell}\mathcal{M}\mathsf{od}_{\mathsf{r}}),

and similarly on
\mathcal{D}\mathsf{}_{\mathsf{inter}}
which produce the actual modules of systems.

For a symmetric monoidal object
T
of
\mathcal{D}\mathsf{}_{\mathsf{sys}}
,

•

We call
T
a
systems theory
in the doctrine
\mathcal{D}\mathsf{}_{\mathsf{sys}}
.

•

We call
\pi_{\mathcal{D}}(T)
the
interaction theory
for the systems theory
T
.

•

The systems theory
T
defines a module of systems
\mathbb{S}(T)
over thte double category of interactions
\mathbb{I}(\pi_{\mathcal{D}}(T))
.

Explication 5.2

(Doctrine of systems theories)
.

We think of a doctrine
\mathbb{S}\colon\mathcal{D}\mathsf{}_{\mathsf{sys}}\to{\ell}\mathcal{M}\mathsf{od}_{\mathsf{r}}
as a way of answering the questions of
Informal definition
1.1
about what it means
to be a system
.

1.

An object
T\in\mathcal{S}\mathsf{M}(\mathcal{D}\mathsf{}_{\mathsf{sys}})
is a
theory of
\mathcal{D}
-systems
. The object
T
itself is really the underlying
data
needed to specify precise answers to the questions of
Informal definition
1.1
. The object
\pi_{\mathcal{D}}(T)\in\mathcal{S}\mathsf{M}(\mathcal{D}\mathsf{}_{\mathsf{inter}})
is the data needed to specify the interactions.

2.

The pseudo-functor
\mathbb{S}
then takes this data
T
and produces a module of systems

\mathbb{S}(T)\colon\bullet\mathrel{\mkern 3.0mu\vbox{\hbox{$\shortmid$}}\mkern-10.0mu{\to}}\mathbb{I}(\pi_{\mathcal{D}}(T))

in which systems are acted on by the symmetric monoidal double category
\mathbb{I}(\pi_{\mathcal{D}}(T))
of interactions.

Remark 5.3

(On the minimal notion of doctrine)
.

Our definition of doctrine is rather minimal. We choose to go with this minimal definition so that it can act as an organizing principle, rather than as a mathematical object of study in its own right (which might require us to discover further structure carried by doctrines of systems theories in particular, above their 2-functoriality). That is to say, we will construct examples of doctrines as defined in
Definition
5.1
, but we will not investigate any higher category of doctrines.

Nevertheless, even with such a minimal definition of doctrine, the notion will help us organize the vast array of systems theories in use by applied category theorists. We may make use of morphisms of doctrines given by precomposition to express that one doctrine is a special case of another. In the upcoming
Section
8
, we will perform a construction at the doctrine level to restrict a doctrine to wiring diagrams or free processes.

5.2
Doctrine of initial processes

Given a symmetric monoidal double category
(\mathbb{D},\otimes,1)
, its

\mathsf{Hom}^{l}(\mathbb{D})\colon\mathbb{D}\mathrel{\mkern 3.0mu\vbox{\hbox{$\shortmid$}}\mkern-10.0mu{\to}}\mathbb{D}

is symmetric monoidal as well. To turn this loose bimodule into a module of systems, we collapse the left action. We do this essentially by giving a
symmetric monoidal niche
,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{D}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{D}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{D}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Hom}^{l}(\mathbb{D})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}

and taking its restriction

\mathsf{Hom}^{l}(\mathbb{D})(1,\mathbb{D})\colon\bullet\mathrel{\mkern 3.0mu\vbox{\hbox{$\shortmid$}}\mkern-10.0mu{\to}}\mathbb{D}.

The constraint that this niche is symmetric monoidal implies that:

•

The map
\bullet\to\mathbb{D}
must map the single object of
\bullet
to
1
.

•

The canonical isomorphism
1\otimes 1\cong 1
is a
conjoint commuter transform
.

This first of these features implies that the systems in the module of systems
\mathsf{Hom}^{l}(\mathbb{D})(1,\mathbb{D})
are loose morphisms
x\colon 1\mathrel{\mkern 3.0mu\vbox{\hbox{$\shortmid$}}\mkern-10.0mu{\to}}d
in
\mathbb{D}
. These systems are acted on by loose morphisms in
\mathbb{D}
.

The second of these features allows us to take the monoidal product of systems in the module of systems theory that is the restriction of this niche. In particular, given two systems
x\colon 1\mathrel{\mkern 3.0mu\vbox{\hbox{$\shortmid$}}\mkern-10.0mu{\to}}d
and
x^{\prime}\colon 1\mathrel{\mkern 3.0mu\vbox{\hbox{$\shortmid$}}\mkern-10.0mu{\to}}d^{\prime}
, their monoidal product
in
\mathbb{D}
is the loose morphism
x\otimes x^{\prime}\colon 1\otimes 1\mathrel{\mkern 3.0mu\vbox{\hbox{$\shortmid$}}\mkern-10.0mu{\to}}d\otimes d^{\prime}
. Notably, this is not a system in the systems theory
\mathsf{Hom}^{l}(\mathbb{D})(1,\mathbb{D})
, because its domain is not
1
. However, we can apply the conjoint commuter transform of the isomorphism
1\otimes 1\cong 1
to modify
x\otimes x^{\prime}
so that its domain is
1
and hence is a system in this systems theory.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{1\otimes 1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{d\otimes d^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x\otimes x^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}

In the remainder of this section, we define a
doctrine of initial processes
. A
theory
in this doctrine will be a symmetric monoidal double category
(\mathbb{D},\otimes,1)
whose canonical isomorphism
1\otimes 1\cong 1
is a
conjoint commuter transform
and it will define the module of systems that is the restriction of the niche shown above.

We begin by defining a 2-category of pointed double categories, whose symmetric monoidal objects objects will be the
theories
of the doctrine of initial processes.

Definition 5.4

(Pointed double category)
.

A
pointed double category
is a double category
\mathbb{D}
equipped with an object
d_{\bullet}\in\mathbb{D}
. Equivalently, it is a double functor
d_{\bullet}:\bullet\to\mathbb{D}
from the terminal double category.

Definition 5.5

(The cartesian 2-category of pointed double functors)
.

The
2-category
\mathcal{D}\mathsf{bl}_{\bullet}
of pointed double categories
and double functors which preserve the point up to a
conjoint tight isomorphism
is defined to be the following pullback:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{D}\mathsf{bl}_{\bullet}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathsf{2}\mathcal{C}\mathsf{at}^{\mathsf{colax}}(\Delta[1],\mathcal{D}\mathsf{bl})_{\mathsf{conj}}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{D}\mathsf{bl}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{D}\mathsf{bl}\times\mathcal{D}\mathsf{bl}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(d_{1}^{*},d_{0}^{*})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\bullet,\mathrm{id})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

which, as a pullback of cartesian 2-functors, is a cartesian 2-category.

Lemma 5.6

(Symmetric monoidal and cartesian objects of
\mathcal{D}\mathsf{bl}_{\bullet}
)
.

A symmetric monoidal object of
the 2-category
\mathcal{D}\mathsf{bl}_{\bullet}
of pointed double categories
is equivalent to a symmetric monoidal double category
\mathbb{D}
for which the canonical isomorphism
1\otimes 1\cong 1
is a
conjoint commuter cell
.

Similarly, a cartesian object of
\mathcal{D}\mathsf{bl}_{\bullet}
is a cartesian double category for which the unique isomorphism
1\times 1\to 1
is a
conjoint commuter cell
.

Proof.

First, note that the point of a symmetric monoidal pointed double category is its identity object.

The remainder follows by
symmetry of internalization
(Theorem 7.4 of
[
ABK24
]
): forgetting for a moment the conjoint commuter condition in
\mathcal{D}\mathsf{bl}_{\bullet}
, we would have that
\mathcal{S}\mathsf{M}(\mathcal{D}\mathsf{bl}_{\bullet})\cong\ast\downarrow^{\mathsf{ps}}\mathcal{S}\mathsf{M}(\mathcal{D}\mathsf{bl})
is the pseudo-slice under the terminal symmetric monoidal category. But a symmetric monoidal double functor from the terminal symmetric monoidal double category must be (up to isomorphism given by the unitor of that functor) be an inclusion of the monoidal unit; since
\mathcal{D}\mathsf{bl}_{\bullet}
does in fact require the conjoint commuter condition, we see that the laxator of the inclusion of the monoidal unit must be a conjoint commuter.

A similar argument applies in the cartesian case.∎

We can now describe the doctrine of initial processes. First, by defining
right niches
and their restriction to looser right modules and then giving a doctrine that factors through this restriction.

Definition 5.7

(The 2-category of right niches)
.

A
right niche
is a niche whose left leg is a pseudo double functor out of the terminal double category:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{E}_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{M_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{M_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}

There is a 2-category of right niches
\mathcal{N}\mathsf{iche}_{r}
which is the pullback of the following 2-categories.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{N}\mathsf{iche}_{r}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{N}\mathsf{iche}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\ell}\mathcal{B}\mathsf{imod}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{D}\mathsf{bl}_{\bullet}\times\mathsf{2}\mathcal{C}\mathsf{at}^{\mathsf{colax}}(\Delta[1],\mathcal{D}\mathsf{bl})_{\mathsf{comp}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{2}\mathcal{C}\mathsf{at}^{\mathsf{colax}}(\Delta[1],\mathcal{D}\mathsf{bl})_{\mathsf{conj}}\times\mathsf{2}\mathcal{C}\mathsf{at}^{\mathsf{colax}}(\Delta[1],\mathcal{D}\mathsf{bl})_{\mathsf{comp}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{D}\mathsf{bl}\times\mathcal{D}\mathsf{bl}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(()_{0},()_{1})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{0}^{*}\times d_{0}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

Since
\mathcal{N}\mathsf{iche}_{r}
is the pullback of cartesian 2-categories along cartesian 2-functors, it is cartesian as is the restriction

\mathsf{Res}\colon\mathcal{N}\mathsf{iche}_{r}\to{\ell}\mathcal{M}\mathsf{od}_{\mathsf{r}}.

Proposition 5.8

(Restriction of right niches)
.

There is a cartesian 2-functor
\mathsf{Res}\colon\mathcal{N}\mathsf{iche}\to{\ell}\mathcal{M}\mathsf{od}_{\mathsf{r}}
that makes the following diagram commute:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{N}\mathsf{iche}_{r}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\ell}\mathcal{M}\mathsf{od}_{\mathsf{r}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{N}\mathsf{iche}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\ell}\mathcal{B}\mathsf{imod}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Res}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Res}}

On objects this implies that the restriction of a right niche is a loose right module.

Proof.

The outer diagram in the following commutes by
Proposition
3.19
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{N}\mathsf{iche}_{r}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{N}\mathsf{iche}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{D}\mathsf{bl}_{\bullet}\times\mathsf{2}\mathcal{C}\mathsf{at}^{\mathsf{colax}}(\Delta[1],\mathcal{D}\mathsf{bl})_{\mathsf{comp}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\ell}\mathcal{M}\mathsf{od}_{\mathsf{r}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\ell}\mathcal{B}\mathsf{imod}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{D}\mathsf{bl}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{D}\mathsf{bl}\times\mathcal{D}\mathsf{bl}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Res}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Res}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{1}^{*}\circ\pi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(()_{0},()_{1})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\bullet,\mathrm{id})}

So
\mathsf{Res}\colon\mathcal{N}\mathsf{iche}\to{\ell}\mathcal{M}\mathsf{od}_{\mathsf{r}}
is induced by the universal property of pullback. Furthermore, as the pullback of cartesian 2-functors, it is cartesian as well.∎

Proposition 5.9

(The right niche of a pointed double category)
.

There exists a cartesian 2-functor
p\colon\mathcal{D}\mathsf{bl}_{\bullet}\to\mathcal{N}\mathsf{iche}_{r}
that takes a pointed double category
d_{\bullet}\colon\bullet\to\mathbb{D}
to the right niche

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{D}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{D}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{D}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{\bullet}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Hom}^{l}(\mathbb{D})}

Proof.

Let
\pi\colon\mathcal{D}\mathsf{bl}_{\bullet}\to\mathcal{D}\mathsf{bl}
be the functor that forgets the point of a pointed double category. Note that on objects,
s_{0}^{*}\colon\mathcal{D}\mathsf{bl}\to\mathsf{2}\mathcal{C}\mathsf{at}^{\mathsf{colax}}(\Delta[1],\mathcal{D}\mathsf{bl})_{\mathsf{comp}}
takes a double category to the identity double functor on it. Then, the following diagram of cartesian 2-functors commutes and induces the desired cartesian 2-functor.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{D}\mathsf{bl}_{\bullet}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{D}\mathsf{bl}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{N}\mathsf{iche}_{r}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\ell}\mathcal{B}\mathsf{imod}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{D}\mathsf{bl}_{\bullet}\times\mathsf{2}\mathcal{C}\mathsf{at}^{\mathsf{colax}}(\Delta[1],\mathcal{D}\mathsf{bl})_{\mathsf{comp}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{D}\mathsf{bl}\times\mathcal{D}\mathsf{bl}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathrm{id},s_{0}^{*}\pi)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Hom}^{l}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(()_{0},()_{1})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi\times d_{0}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

∎

Definition 5.10

(Doctrine of initial processes)
.

The
doctrine of initial processes
is given by the following commuting square of cartesian 2-functors:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{D}\mathsf{bl}_{\bullet}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{N}\mathsf{iche}_{r}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\ell}\mathcal{M}\mathsf{od}_{\mathsf{r}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{D}\mathsf{bl}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{D}\mathsf{bl}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Res}}

where
p\colon\mathcal{D}\mathsf{bl}_{\bullet}\to\mathcal{N}\mathsf{iche}_{r}
is defined in
Proposition
5.9
.

Applying
\mathcal{S}\mathsf{M}
to this doctrine, we see that
symmetric monoindal objects of
\mathcal{D}\mathsf{bl}_{\bullet}
are theories that produce modules of systems. Finally, we verify that these theories are indeed what we claimed at the beginning of this section.

Explication 5.11

(Doctrine of initial processes)
.

Explicitly, the
doctrine of initial processes
sends a pointed double category
d_{\bullet}:\bullet\to\mathbb{D}
to the loose bimodule
\mathsf{Hom}^{l}(\mathbb{D})(d_{\bullet},\mathbb{D}):\bullet\mathrel{\mkern 3.0mu\vbox{\hbox{$\shortmid$}}\mkern-10.0mu{\to}}\mathbb{D}
of its
loose Hom bimodule
. In particular, the objects of its
carrier
are loose morphisms
d_{\bullet}\mathrel{\mkern 3.0mu\vbox{\hbox{$\shortmid$}}\mkern-10.0mu{\to}}d
. The loose morphisms of its
target
are loose morphisms of
\mathbb{D}
which act by composition.

Recall, that systems theories are symmetric monoidal objects of the doctrine’s domain. By
Lemma
5.6
the symmetric monoidal objects of
\mathcal{D}\mathsf{bl}_{\bullet}
are, essentially, just the symmetric monoidal double categories (satisfying a mild condition) pointed by their unit.

Therefore, when we start with a symmetric monoidal double category
\mathbb{P}
(which we refer to as a
process theory
) and pass it through the doctrine of initial processes, we end up with a module of systems over
\mathbb{P}
whose systems are loose homs out of its monoidal unit, which we call
initial processes
.

6
Span and cospan doctrines via adequate triples

In this section, we will further specify the
doctrine of initial processes
includes many of our examples, we now turn to more specific doctrines which have a more uniform flavor. The remaining constructions will all use the same underlying categorical technology:
spans
.

Some systems theories — those that compose by
sharing variables
like the Hamiltonian and Langranian mechanics of
[
BWY21
]
(see our similar construction, following 1.3.2 of
[
Sch
]
in
Example
2.19
) and the Schultz-Spivak-Vasilakopolou approach to Willems’ style behavioral control theory through sheaves (
machines
in
[
SSV19
]
) — are naturally described by spans and pullback. But many others — those which compose by
gluing
together subsystems such as Petri nets
[
BM20
]
and stock-flow diagrams
[
Bae+22
]
— are better handled by cospans, as well explored by the decorated and structured cospan literature (see, e.g.
[
Cou20
]
). Cospans are, however, just spans in the opposite category.

We begin by defining a doctrine of spans of an adequate triple, which factors through the
doctrine of initial processes
. We then define doctrines of spans of lex categories and cospans of rex categories, which factor through the doctrine of spans of adequate triples. Respectively, these correspond to the doctrines of variables sharing and port-plugging.

6.1
Doctrine of spans of adequate triples

We begin by defining the cartesian category of
pointed adequate triples
. Symmetric monoidal objects in this category will be theories in the doctrine of spans of adequate triples.

Definition 6.1

(Pointed adequate triple)
.

A
pointed adequate triple
consists of an
adequate triple

(\mathsf{C},(L,R))
equipped with an object
c\in\mathsf{C}
thought of as a map from the terminal adequate triple
c:(\bullet,(\mathsf{all},\mathsf{all}))\to(\mathsf{C},(L,R))
.

The 2-category
\mathcal{A}\mathsf{dTr}_{\bullet}
of pointed adequate triples consists of maps of adequate triples which preserve the point up to an isomorphism in the left class. That is, we may define
\mathcal{A}\mathsf{dTr}_{\bullet}
as the full sub-2-category of the pseudo-slice
\bullet\downarrow^{\mathsf{ps}}\mathcal{A}\mathsf{dTr}
under the terminal adequate triple, itself constructed by the following pullback:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet\downarrow^{\mathsf{ps}}\mathcal{A}\mathsf{dTr}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{2}\mathcal{C}\mathsf{at}^{\mathsf{ps}}(\Delta[1],\mathcal{A}\mathsf{dTr})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}\mathsf{dTr}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{A}\mathsf{dTr}\times\mathcal{A}\mathsf{dTr}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(d_{1}^{\ast},d_{0}^{\ast})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\bullet,\mathrm{id})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

consisting of those 1-cells

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{D}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

whose colaxator isomorphism
\ell
is in the left class.

Observation 6.2

(Extending the span construction to pointed adequate triples)
.

The
span
cartesian 2-functor
\mathbb{S}\mathsf{pan}:\mathcal{A}\mathsf{dTr}\to\mathcal{D}\mathsf{bl}
extends to a cartesian 2-functor
\mathbb{S}\mathsf{pan}:\mathcal{A}\mathsf{dTr}\to\mathcal{D}\mathsf{bl}
which maps the adequate triple
(\mathsf{C},(L,R))
with point
c\in\mathsf{C}
to the double category
\mathbb{S}\mathsf{pan}(\mathsf{C},(L,R))
with point
c
as well.

We note that
\mathbb{S}\mathsf{pan}_{\bullet}
does indeed land in
\mathcal{D}\mathsf{bl}_{\bullet}
since we assumed that the isomorphisms witnessing preservation of the pointings were in the left class, and these are all conjoints in
\mathbb{S}\mathsf{pan}(\mathsf{C},(L,R))
.

Finally, we may then restrict the to
pointed adequate triples
along the
span construction
to get a doctrine of spans in an adequate triple.

Definition 6.3

(Doctrine of spans in a pointed adequate triple)
.

By restricting the , we get the following doctrine of spans in a pointed adequate triple.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}\mathsf{dTr}_{\bullet}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{D}\mathsf{bl}_{\bullet}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{N}\mathsf{iche}_{r}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\ell}\mathcal{M}\mathsf{od}_{\mathsf{r}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}\mathsf{dTr}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{D}\mathsf{bl}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{D}\mathsf{bl}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{S}\mathsf{pan}_{\bullet}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Res}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces()_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{S}\mathsf{pan}}

Explication 6.4

(Doctrine of spans in a pointed adequate triple)
.

In the
doctrine of spans in a pointed adequate triple
, a theory is a symmetric monoidal pointed adequate triple. As in
the case of pointed double categories
, a symmetric monoidal object of
\mathcal{A}\mathsf{dTr}_{\bullet}
consists of a
symmetric monoidal adequate triple

(\mathsf{C},(L,R))
whose point is the monoidal object
1
of
\mathsf{C}
.

This theory produces the module of systems

\mathbb{S}\mathsf{pan}(\mathsf{C},(L,R))(1,-)\colon\bullet\mathrel{\mkern 3.0mu\vbox{\hbox{$\shortmid$}}\mkern-10.0mu{\to}}\mathbb{S}\mathsf{pan}(\mathsf{C},(L,R)).

This module of systems has:

•

An interface is an object of
\mathsf{C}
.

•

A system with interface
c
is a span
1\xleftarrow{\ell\in L}x\xrightarrow{r\in R}c
.

•

An interaction is a span in the adequate triple which acts on systems by pullback.

•

Maps of systems and maps of interactions are maps of spans.

6.2
Span and cospan doctrines for lex and rex categories

We now give two specific examples of this doctrine: the
variable sharing
doctrine of spans in a
lex category
and the
port-plugging
doctrine of cospans in a
rex category
.

We begin by defining pointed lex and rex categories, whose symmetric monoidal objects will be
theories
in these doctrines.

Lemma 6.5

(The pointed adequate triple of a lex (resp. rex) category)
.

The cartesian 2-functor
\mathcal{L}\mathsf{ex}\to\mathcal{A}\mathsf{dTr}
defined in
Construction
2.17
extends to a cartesian 2-functor
\mathcal{L}\mathsf{ex}\to\mathcal{A}\mathsf{dTr}_{\bullet}
which on objects sends a lex category
\mathsf{C}
to the adquate triple
(\mathsf{C},(\mathsf{all},\mathsf{all}))
pointed by the initial object
1\in\mathsf{C}
.

Likewise, the cartesian 2-functor
{\mathcal{R}\mathsf{ex}}^{\mathsf{co}}\to\mathcal{A}\mathsf{dTr}
defined in
Construction
2.23
extends to a cartesian 2-functor
{\mathcal{R}\mathsf{ex}}^{\mathsf{co}}\to\mathcal{A}\mathsf{dTr}_{\bullet}
which on objects sends a rex category
\mathsf{C}
to the adquate triple
(\mathsf{C},(\mathsf{all},\mathsf{all}))
pointed by the terminal object
0\in\mathsf{C}
.

Proof.

This follows from the definition of
pointed adequate triples
and the fact that lex categories preserve initial objects and hence the lex functor from the terminal lex category to a lex category
\mathsf{C}
picks out the initial object of
\mathsf{C}
. Likewise for rex functors from the terminal rex category to a rex category
\mathsf{C}
picks out the terminal object of
\mathsf{C}
.∎

Observation 6.6

(Symmetric monoidal pointed lex and rex categories)
.

We note that
\mathcal{L}\mathsf{ex}
has biproducts, so that every lex category is uniquely symmetric monoidal with the symmetric monoidal product given by cartesian product.

Likewise,
\mathcal{R}\mathsf{ex}
has biproducts and so every rex category is uniquely symmetric monoidal with the symmetric monoidal product given by coproduct.

We can restrict the
adequate triple doctrine
along the inclusion
\mathcal{L}\mathsf{ex}\to\mathcal{A}\mathsf{dTr}
of
Construction
2.17
. We will call the resulting doctrine the
variable sharing
doctrine.

Definition 6.7

(Variable sharing doctrine)
.

We define the
variable sharing

doctrine
to be the restriction of the
doctrine of spans of adequate triples
to
adequate triples induced by lex categories
, we get the following doctrine:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{L}\mathsf{ex}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}\mathsf{dTr}_{\bullet}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{D}\mathsf{bl}_{\bullet}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{N}\mathsf{iche}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\ell}\mathcal{M}\mathsf{od}_{\mathsf{r}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{L}\mathsf{ex}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}\mathsf{dTr}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{D}\mathsf{bl}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{D}\mathsf{bl}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{S}\mathsf{pan}_{\bullet}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Res}}
1
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{S}\mathsf{pan}}

Definition 6.8

(Port-plugging doctrine)
.

We define the
port-plugging

doctrine
to be the restriction of the
span doctrine of a pointed adequate triple
to
adequate triples induced by rex categories
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{R}\mathsf{ex}^{\mathsf{co}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{L}\mathsf{ex}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}\mathsf{dTr}_{\bullet}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{D}\mathsf{bl}_{\bullet}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{N}\mathsf{iche}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\ell}\mathcal{M}\mathsf{od}_{\mathsf{r}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{R}\mathsf{ex}^{\mathsf{co}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{L}\mathsf{ex}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}\mathsf{dTr}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{D}\mathsf{bl}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{D}\mathsf{bl}}
op
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{S}\mathsf{pan}_{\bullet}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Res}}
1
op
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{S}\mathsf{pan}}

This sends a rex category
\mathsf{C}
to the loose right module

\mathbb{C}\mathsf{ospan}(\mathsf{C})^{\mathsf{op}}(\emptyset,-)\colon\bullet\mathrel{\mkern 3.0mu\vbox{\hbox{$\shortmid$}}\mkern-10.0mu{\to}}\mathbb{C}\mathsf{ospan}(\mathsf{C})^{\mathsf{op}}

of cospans out of the initial object. We can further dualize (in the tight direction) to get

\mathbb{C}\mathsf{ospan}(\mathsf{C})(\emptyset,-)\colon\bullet\mathrel{\mkern 3.0mu\vbox{\hbox{$\shortmid$}}\mkern-10.0mu{\to}}\mathbb{C}\mathsf{ospan}(\mathsf{C})

as a pseudo-functor of
\mathsf{C}\in\mathcal{R}\mathsf{ex}
(rather than
\mathcal{R}\mathsf{ex}^{\mathsf{co}}
).

7
The doctrine of generalized Moore machines

In
Section
6
, we saw how systems that compose via
variable sharing
or
port plugging
are defined by theories in doctrines defined by spans and cospans.

Other systems — those which are
generalized Moore machines
, such as systems of ODEs and POMDPs and other "automata" — compose best via
lenses
, as described for example in
[
VSL14
]
(see also
[
Mye21
]
for a pedagogical overview). However, lenses may also be described as spans of a particular sort (see
Definition
2.35
).

Roughly, a generalized Moore machine consists of an internal state, an input, and an output. Outputs are determined by the state and states change according to input. In the doctrine of generalized Moore machines that we will describe in this section, a theory (i.e. the data needed to define a module of systems) is a
tangency
, which consists of:

•

A notion of
space
.

•

A notion of
bundle
over spaces, which may be pulled back.

•

An assignment of a "tangent" bundle to each space.

A tangency is the data necessary to construct a module of systems for which systems are
generalized Moore machines
and interactions are
lenses
(in the generalized sense of Spivak
[
Spi19
]
).

With this data in place, we can now sketch the definition a generalized Moore machine following the discussion in
[
Jaz21
]
. A generalized Moore machine consists of:

•

A way things may be in the form of a space
S
of
states
.

•

A way things may change in the form of an element
TS
in the fiber over
S
. In many examples of a tangency, such an element is in fact a bundle
TS\to S
. Then, for a state
s\in S
, the fiber
T_{s}S
represents possible changes from
s
.

•

An
interface
in the form of:

–

A space
O
of
observations
that may be made of the system or
orientations
which the system may take in its environment.

–

An element
I
in the fiber over
O
of
inputs
. In many examples of a tangency, such an element is in fact a bundle
I\to O
. For an orientation
o\in O
, and the space
I_{o}
represents
parameters
to the dynamics of the system which are available in that orientation.

•

An
observation

e\colon S\to O
of the system, which exposes some aspects of state.

•

A
(parameterized) dynamics

u\colon e^{\ast}I\to TS
. In many examples of a tangency, these dynamics assign a state
s\in S
and a valid parameter
p\in I_{e(s)}
, to a change
u_{s}(p)\in T_{s}S
.

7.1
Tangencies

In this section, we give a definition of symmetric monoidal tangencies, which are the theories in the doctrine of generalized Moore machines.

Definition 7.1

(The 2-category of tangencies)
.

A
tangency
is a
cartesian fibration

\pi:E\to B
equipped with a section
T:B\to E
(with
\pi T=\mathrm{id}_{B}
).

The 2-category
\mathcal{T}\mathsf{an}
of tangencies is defined as the following pullback of 2-categories:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{T}\mathsf{an}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{2}\mathcal{C}\mathsf{at}^{\mathsf{colax}}(\Delta[2],\mathcal{C}\mathsf{at})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}\mathsf{ib}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{2\mathcal{C}\mathsf{at}(\Delta[1],\mathcal{C}\mathsf{at})\times\mathcal{C}\mathsf{at}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{2}\mathcal{C}\mathsf{at}^{\mathsf{colax}}(\Delta[1],\mathcal{C}\mathsf{at})\times\mathsf{2}\mathcal{C}\mathsf{at}^{\mathsf{colax}}(\Delta[1],\mathcal{C}\mathsf{at})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(d_{0}^{\ast},d_{1}^{\ast})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(U,d_{0}^{\ast})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\iota,s_{0}^{*})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

where
U\colon\mathcal{F}\mathsf{ib}\to 2\mathcal{C}\mathsf{at}(\Delta[1],\mathcal{C}\mathsf{at})
forgets the structure of the fibration,
\iota\colon 2\mathcal{C}\mathsf{at}(\Delta[1],\mathcal{C}\mathsf{at})\to\mathsf{2}\mathcal{C}\mathsf{at}^{\mathsf{colax}}(\Delta[1],\mathcal{D}\mathsf{bl})
is the inclusion, and
s_{0}^{*}\colon\mathcal{C}\mathsf{at}\to\mathsf{2}\mathcal{C}\mathsf{at}^{\mathsf{colax}}(\Delta[1],\mathcal{D}\mathsf{bl})
takes a category to its identity functor.

Explication 7.2

(The 2-category of tangencies)
.

Here we explicitly state the objects, morphisms, and 2-cells of
\mathcal{T}\mathsf{an}
.

•

An object is a tangency. In other words, a pair
(\pi\colon E\to B,T\colon B\to E)
where
\pi
is a cartesian fibration and
T
is a section of
\pi
.

•

A morphism of tangencies

((f,\overline{f}),\phi)\colon(\pi_{1},T_{1})\to(\pi_{2},T_{2})

consists of a
cartesian functor

(f,\overline{f}):\pi_{1}\to\pi_{2}
together with a colax morphism of sections
\phi:\overline{f}\circ T_{1}\Rightarrow T_{2}\circ f
so that
\pi_{2}\phi=\mathrm{id}_{f}
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{=}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

These compose by whiskering:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B_{3}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B_{3}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E_{3}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{:=}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E_{3}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B_{3}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B_{3}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g\circ f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi;\psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{g}\circ\overline{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{g}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g\circ f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

•

A 2-cell of tangency morphisms is a 2-cell
(\alpha,\overline{\alpha}):(f,\overline{f})\Rightarrow(g,\overline{g})
of
\mathcal{F}\mathsf{ib}
for which

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{=}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{g}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{f}}

Attitude 7.3

(Tangencies as states and possible changes)
.

A
tangency
is a cartesian fibration
\pi:E\to B
equipped with a section
T:B\to E
. We think of a tangency as follows:

1.

B
is a category of
state spaces
.

2.

For every state space
S\in B
, the fiber of
\pi:E\to B
over
S
is a category
E_{S}
of
bundles
over
S
, which can be pulled-back along maps of spaces.

3.

The section
T:B\to E
assigns to every state space its "tangent bundle", or bundle of possible changes.

A
system
in a tangency is then a "generalized Moore machine", or a
lens

{u\choose E}\colon{TS\choose S}\mathrel{\mkern 3.0mu\vbox{\hbox{$\shortmid$}}\mkern-10.0mu{\to}}{I\choose O}
which corresponds to the following span:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TS}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{e^{\ast}I}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces O}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{lift}(e)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}

We think of this as consisting of a morphism
e:S\to O
in
B
which
exposes
some variables of state, and a parameterized update
u:e^{\ast}I\to TS
. Using
lens notation
, we notate such a system a lens

{u\choose e}\colon{TS\choose S}\mathrel{\mkern 3.0mu\vbox{\hbox{$\shortmid$}}\mkern-10.0mu{\to}}{I\choose O}.

We refer the reader to
[
Jaz21
]
for further discussion.

Attitude 7.4

(Morphisms of tangencies as changing the state space)
.

Let
(\pi_{1},T_{1})
and
(\pi_{2},T_{2})
be tangencies and let

((f,\overline{f})\colon\pi_{1}\to\pi_{2},\phi\colon\overline{f}\circ T_{1}\Rightarrow T_{2}\circ f)

be
a morphism of tangencies
.
We interpret this morphism as transforming the state space
S
in the tangency
(\pi_{1},T_{1})
into the state space
f(S)
in the tangency
(\pi_{2},T_{2})
. Simply applying
\overline{f}
to the bundle of changes
T_{1}S
over
S
is
not
the bundle of changes
T_{2}(fS)
over
f(S)
. However, applying
\phi_{S}
to
\overline{f}(T_{1}S)
maps it into the bundle of changes
T_{2}(fS)
over
f(S)
.

We can interpret this transformation as a lens. For
S\in B_{1}
, the morphism
\phi_{S}
in
E_{2}
defines the following lens in
\mathbb{L}\mathsf{ens}(E_{2})

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T_{2}(fS)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\overline{f}(T_{1}S)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\overline{f}(T_{1}S)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f(S)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f(S)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f(S)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{S}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{2}}

Using
lens notation
,
\phi
induces lenses

{\phi_{S}\choose\mathrm{id}}\colon{T_{2}(fS)\choose f(S)}\mathrel{\mkern 3.0mu\vbox{\hbox{$\shortmid$}}\mkern-10.0mu{\to}}{\overline{f}(T_{1}S)\choose f(S)}.

See Proposition 4.5.1.11 of the manuscript
[
Mye21
]
to see the Euler method as an example of a morphism of tangencies from a tangency of ODEs to a tangency for discrete-time, continuous-space Moore machines. We expect that the Runge-Kutta method could also be expressed as such morphism, allowing us to derive the compositionality theorem proved by Ngotiaoco
[
Ngo17
]
via the pseudo-functoriality of the tangency
doctrine
.

Remark 7.5

(Tangencies in
[
Mye21
]
)
.

The definition of a tangency in the context of categorical systems theory first appears as a "dynamical systems doctrine" in Definition 1.1 of
[
Jaz21
]
. It appears further in
[
Mye21
]
as a "theory of dynamical systems" Definition 3.5.0.4 and Definition 4.5.1.2 respectively.

Here, we have changed the terminology to "tangency" to accomodate the other theories of dynamical systems we find in other doctrines. We also finally add the symmetric monoidal structure necessary for the parallel product of systems; see
Lemma
7.7
.

Finally, we show that
\mathcal{T}\mathsf{an}
is cartesian and characterize its symmetric monoidal objects. These are the theories in the doctrine of generalized Moore machines.

Lemma 7.6

(Tangencies form a cartesian 2-category)
.

The 2-category
\mathcal{T}\mathsf{an}
of
tangencies
form a cartesian 2-category with products constructed in
\mathcal{F}\mathsf{ib}
(and therefore ultimately in
\mathcal{C}\mathsf{at}
).

Proof.

The product of
(\pi_{1},T_{1})
and
(\pi_{2},T_{2})
is
(\pi_{1}\times\pi_{2},T_{1}\times T_{2})
. We equip the projections with the identity transformations
p_{i}(T_{1}\times T_{2})=T_{i}p_{i}
. It is straightforward to verify the 2-dimensional universal property of the product, since the requisite equations may be checked componentwise.∎

Lemma 7.7

(Symmetric monoidal tangency)
.

By
symmetry of internalization
(Theorem 7.4 of
[
ABK24
]
), a symmetric monoidal tangency
(\pi,T)\in\mathcal{S}\mathsf{M}(\mathcal{T}\mathsf{an})
is equivalently a symmetric monoidal fibration
\pi:E\to B
equipped with a lax monoidal section
T:B\to E
.

7.2
Tangencies as systems theories

In this section, we construct a loose bimodule from a tangency. We then this loose bimodule to form a module of systems.

Lemma 7.8

(From tangencies to adequate triples)
.

Let
(\pi\colon E\to B,T\colon B\to E)
be a
tangency
. The section
T
induces a map of
adequate triples
by

T\colon(B,(\mathsf{id},\mathsf{id}))\to(E,(\mathsf{vert},\mathsf{cart}))

from the to the
adequate triple associated to the fibration
\pi
. This construction gives a cartesian 2-functor

\mathsf{T}:\mathcal{T}\mathsf{an}\to\mathsf{2}\mathcal{C}\mathsf{at}^{\mathsf{colax}}(\Delta[1],\mathcal{A}\mathsf{dTr}).

into maps of adequate triples and colax transformations.

Proof.

The underlying 2-functor into
\mathcal{C}\mathsf{at}
is just a projection:

\mathcal{T}\mathsf{an}\to\mathsf{2}\mathcal{C}\mathsf{at}^{\mathsf{colax}}(\Delta[2],\mathcal{C}\mathsf{at})\xrightarrow{d_{2}^{\ast}}\mathsf{2}\mathcal{C}\mathsf{at}^{\mathsf{colax}}(\Delta[1],\mathcal{C}\mathsf{at})

It only remains to show that this lifts along the forgetful functor
\mathsf{2}\mathcal{C}\mathsf{at}^{\mathsf{colax}}(\Delta[1],\mathcal{A}\mathsf{dTr})\to\mathsf{2}\mathcal{C}\mathsf{at}^{\mathsf{colax}}(\Delta[1],\mathcal{C}\mathsf{at})
. Since
T:B\to E
gives a map of adequate triples
T:(B,(\mathsf{id},\mathsf{id}))\to(E,(\mathsf{vert},\mathsf{cart}))
, and since both the assignments
B\mapsto(B(\mathsf{id},\mathsf{id}))
and
\pi\mapsto(E,(\mathsf{vert},\mathsf{cart}))
are 2-functorial, and since the forgetful functor
\mathcal{A}\mathsf{dTr}\to\mathcal{C}\mathsf{at}
is locally fully faithful, we have demonstrated that the above 2-functor lifts to
\mathsf{2}\mathcal{C}\mathsf{at}^{\mathsf{colax}}(\Delta[1],\mathcal{A}\mathsf{dTr})
.

Since the projection is evidently cartesian, and since
products in
\mathcal{A}\mathsf{dTr}
are constructed in
\mathcal{C}\mathsf{at}
, the 2-functor
\mathsf{T}
is cartesian.∎

Lemma 7.9

(Span construction of tangency lands in commuter tranformations)
.

The composite 2-functor

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{T}\mathsf{an}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{2}\mathcal{C}\mathsf{at}^{\mathsf{colax}}(\Delta[1],\mathcal{A}\mathsf{dTr})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{2}\mathcal{C}\mathsf{at}^{\mathsf{colax}}(\Delta[1],\mathcal{D}\mathsf{bl})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{2}\mathcal{C}\mathsf{at}^{\mathsf{colax}}(\Delta[1],\mathbb{S}\mathsf{pan})}

lands in
\mathsf{2}\mathcal{C}\mathsf{at}^{\mathsf{colax}}(\Delta[1],\mathcal{D}\mathsf{bl})_{\mathsf{conj}}
. We will refer to the resulting composite as

\mathbb{S}\mathsf{pan}(\mathsf{T}):\mathcal{T}\mathsf{an}\to\mathsf{2}\mathcal{C}\mathsf{at}^{\mathsf{colax}}(\Delta[1],\mathcal{D}\mathsf{bl})_{\mathsf{conj}}.

Proof.

We need to show that for any 1-cell
\phi:T_{2}f\Rightarrow\overline{f}T_{1}:\pi_{1}\to\pi_{2}
of
tangencies
, the resulting tight transformation
\mathbb{S}\mathsf{pan}(\phi)
is a
conjoint commuter transformation
. Note that by definition,
\phi
is vertical; it is therefore in the left class of the adequate triple
(E_{2},(\mathsf{vert},\mathsf{cart}))
and is
therefore component-wise a conjoint
in
\mathbb{S}\mathsf{pan}(E_{2},(\mathsf{vert},\mathsf{cart}))
. It remains to show that it is a commuter; but the only loose morphisms in
\mathbb{S}\mathsf{pan}(B_{1},(\mathsf{id},\mathsf{id}))
are identities, and the transpose of the loose identity of a conjoint is always a tight isomorphism.∎

Explication 7.10

(From tangencies to spans)
.

Let’s examine the action of the functor
\mathbb{S}\mathsf{pan}(\mathsf{T}):\mathcal{T}\mathsf{an}\to\mathsf{2}\mathcal{C}\mathsf{at}^{\mathsf{colax}}(\Delta[1],\mathcal{D}\mathsf{bl})_{\mathsf{conj}}
on objects.

Let
(\pi\colon E\to B,T\colon B\to E)
be a tangency. We perform the
span construction
to get a map

\mathbb{S}\mathsf{pan}(T):\mathbb{S}\mathsf{pan}(B,(\mathsf{id},\mathsf{id}))\to\mathbb{S}\mathsf{pan}(E,(\mathsf{vert},\mathsf{cart})).

Note that:

•

By
Observation
2.25
,
\mathbb{S}\mathsf{pan}(B,(\mathsf{id},\mathsf{id}))
is the loosely discrete double category
\mathbb{T}\mathsf{}(B)
on
B
which has
B
as its tight category and only identity loose arrows.

•

As per
Definition
2.35
(see also
Explication
2.37
),
\mathbb{S}\mathsf{pan}(E,(\mathsf{vert},\mathsf{cart}))
is the double category
\mathbb{L}\mathsf{ens}(E)
of
Spivak lenses
.

Therefore,
\mathbb{S}\mathsf{pan}(T)
is given just by the action of
T
in the tight categories; it sends a "base space"
b\in B
to its "tangent bundle"
Tb\in E
.

To simplify terminology, let’s refer to
\mathbb{S}\mathsf{pan}(T)
by
T
so that under
\mathbb{S}\mathsf{pan}(\mathsf{T})
, the tangency
(\pi,T)
maps to the double functor:

T:\mathbb{T}\mathsf{}(B)\to\mathbb{L}\mathsf{ens}(E).

Lemma 7.11

(From tangencies to niches)
.

There is a cartesian 2-functor
\mathcal{T}\mathsf{an}\to\mathcal{N}\mathsf{iche}
that takes a tangency
(\pi\colon E\to B,T\colon B\to E)
to the niche

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{T}\mathsf{}(B)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{L}\mathsf{ens}(E)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{L}\mathsf{ens}(E)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{L}\mathsf{ens}(E)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{L}\mathsf{ens}(E)(-,-)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}

Proof.

The composite

\mathcal{T}\mathsf{an}\xrightarrow{\mathbb{S}\mathsf{pan}(\mathsf{T})}\mathsf{2}\mathcal{C}\mathsf{at}^{\mathsf{colax}}(\Delta[1],\mathcal{D}\mathsf{bl})_{\mathsf{conj}}\xrightarrow{d_{0}^{*}}\mathcal{D}\mathsf{bl}

takes a tangency with total space
E
to the double category
\mathbb{L}\mathsf{ens}(E)
of
Spviak lenses
.

The outer diagram in the following commutes.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{T}\mathsf{an}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{D}\mathsf{bl}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{2}\mathcal{C}\mathsf{at}^{\mathsf{colax}}(\Delta[1],\mathcal{D}\mathsf{bl})_{\mathsf{conj}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{N}\mathsf{iche}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\ell}\mathcal{B}\mathsf{imod}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{2}\mathcal{C}\mathsf{at}^{\mathsf{colax}}(\Delta[1],\mathcal{D}\mathsf{bl})_{\mathsf{conj}}\times\mathsf{2}\mathcal{C}\mathsf{at}^{\mathsf{colax}}(\Delta[1],\mathcal{D}\mathsf{bl})_{\mathsf{comp}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{D}\mathsf{bl}\times\mathcal{D}\mathsf{bl}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{0}^{*}\circ\mathbb{S}\mathsf{pan}(\mathsf{T})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{S}\mathsf{pan}(\mathsf{T})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Hom}^{l}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathrm{id},s_{0}^{*}d_{0}^{*})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces()_{0},()_{1})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces()_{1}\times()_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

Hence by the universal property of pullbacks, we have the desired map
\mathcal{T}\mathsf{an}\to\mathcal{N}\mathsf{iche}
.∎

Explication 7.12

(From tangencies to loose bimodules)
.

Let
\mathsf{Sys}\colon\mathcal{T}\mathsf{an}\to{\ell}\mathcal{B}\mathsf{imod}
be the composite of the cartesian 2-functor
\mathcal{T}\mathsf{an}\to\mathcal{N}\mathsf{iche}
defined in
Lemma
7.11
with the
\mathsf{Res}\colon\mathcal{N}\mathsf{iche}\to{\ell}\mathcal{B}\mathsf{imod}
turns a tangency
(\pi\colon E\to B,T\colon B\to E)
into the loose bimodule

\mathbb{L}\mathsf{ens}(T-,-)\colon\mathbb{T}\mathsf{}(B)\mathrel{\mkern 3.0mu\vbox{\hbox{$\shortmid$}}\mkern-10.0mu{\to}}\mathbb{L}\mathsf{ens}(E).

We see that a heteromorphism with domain
S\in B
and codomain
{I\choose O}\in\mathbb{L}\mathsf{ens}(E)
of the resulting bimodule is a
lens

{u\choose e}\colon{TS\choose S}\mathrel{\mkern 3.0mu\vbox{\hbox{$\shortmid$}}\mkern-10.0mu{\to}}{I\choose O}
. Explicitly, such a lens consists of
e\colon S\to O
in
B
and
u\colon e^{*}(I)\to TS
in
E
and these form the span

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TS}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{e^{\ast}I}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{O}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{lift}(e)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}

in
E
.
Such a morphism is precisely a
system
in the tangency as described in
Attitude
7.3
.

It is also worth understanding the functoriality of this construction. Let
(\pi_{1},T_{1})
and
(\pi_{2},T_{2})
be tangencies and let

((f,\overline{f})\colon\pi_{1}\to\pi_{2},\phi\colon\overline{f}\circ T_{1}\Rightarrow T_{2}\circ f)

be
a morphism of tangencies
. Recall the
attitude for morphisms of tangencies
The morphism on tangencies induces a (covariant) action on systems which for
S\in B
and
I\in E
sends the system
{u\choose e}\colon{T_{1}S\choose S}\mathrel{\mkern 3.0mu\vbox{\hbox{$\shortmid$}}\mkern-10.0mu{\to}}{I\choose O}
in
\mathbb{L}\mathsf{ens}(E_{1})
to the composite

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{T_{2}(fS)\choose fS}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\overline{f}T_{1}S\choose fS}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\overline{f}I\choose fO}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\phi_{S}\choose\mathrm{id}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\overline{f}u\choose fe}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}

in
\mathbb{L}\mathsf{ens}(E_{2})
. This lens corresponds to the following span:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T_{2}fS}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\overline{f}T_{1}S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\overline{f}(e^{\ast}I)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\overline{f}I}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces fS}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces fS}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces fS}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces fO}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{S}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{f}u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{f}\mathsf{lift}(e)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces fe}

Finally, we are ready to define the doctrine of generalized Moore machines.

Definition 7.13

(Doctrine of generalized Moore machines)
.

We define the
doctrine
of
generalized Moore machines
as follows:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{T}\mathsf{an}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\ell}\mathcal{M}\mathsf{od}_{\mathsf{r}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}\mathsf{ib}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{D}\mathsf{bl}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{M}\mathsf{oore}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{L}\mathsf{ens}}

where
\mathbb{M}\mathsf{oore}
is the composite of the
collapse of a loose bimodule into a right bimodule
of
\mathsf{Sys}
, the
loose bimodule of systems associated to a tangency
. The 2-functor
\mathbb{L}\mathsf{ens}:\mathcal{F}\mathsf{ib}\to\mathcal{D}\mathsf{bl}
is the
lens construction
.

Explication 7.14

(Doctrine of generalized Moore machines)
.

In the
doctrine of generalized Moore machines
, a systems theory is a
symmetric monoidal tangency

(\pi\colon E\to B,T\colon B\to E)
. It produces the module of systems
\mathbb{M}\mathsf{oore}(\pi,T)
over the double category of interactions
\mathbb{L}\mathsf{ens}(E)
.

•

An interface in this module of systems is an object
{I\choose O}
in
\mathbb{L}\mathsf{ens}(E)
.

•

A system with interface
{I\choose O}
consists of a state space
S\in B
and a lens
{u\choose e}\colon{TS\choose S}\mathrel{\mkern 3.0mu\vbox{\hbox{$\shortmid$}}\mkern-10.0mu{\to}}{I\choose O}
in which
e
represents an observation and
u
represents parameterized dynamics.

•

An interaction is a lens that acts on systems via lens composition.

•

Maps of systems and maps of interactions are given by charts.

Next we investigate the monoidal product of systems in this doctrine. For
i=1,2
, let
{u_{i}\choose r_{i}}\colon{TS_{i}\choose S_{i}}\mathrel{\mkern 3.0mu\vbox{\hbox{$\shortmid$}}\mkern-10.0mu{\to}}{I_{i}\choose O_{i}}
be systems in
\mathbb{M}\mathsf{oore}(\pi,T)
. Since
(\pi,T)
is a symmetric monoidal object of
\mathcal{T}\mathsf{an}
, by
symmetry of internalization

T\colon B\to E
has laxator
\mu_{S_{1},S_{2}}:TS_{1}\otimes TS_{2}\Rightarrow T(S_{1}\otimes S_{2})
which defines a lens

{\mu_{S_{1},S_{2}}\choose\mathrm{id}}\colon{T(S_{1}\otimes S_{2})\choose S_{1}\otimes S_{2}}\mathrel{\mkern 3.0mu\vbox{\hbox{$\shortmid$}}\mkern-10.0mu{\to}}{TS_{1}\otimes TS_{2}\choose S_{1}\otimes S_{2}}.

The monoidal product of the systems
{u_{1}\choose r_{1}}
and
{u_{2}\choose r_{2}}
is the composite of lenses

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{T(S_{1}\otimes S_{2})\choose S_{1}\otimes S_{2}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{TS_{1}\otimes TS_{2}\choose S_{1}\otimes S_{2}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{I_{1}\otimes I_{2}\choose O_{1}\otimes O_{2}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mu_{S_{1},S_{2}}\choose\mathrm{id}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{u_{1}\otimes u_{2}\choose e_{1}\otimes e_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}

which corresponds to the following span

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T(S_{1}\otimes S_{2})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{TS_{1}\otimes TS_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{e_{1}^{\ast}I_{1}\otimes e_{2}^{\ast}I_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{I_{1}\otimes I_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{S_{1}\otimes S_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{S_{1}\otimes S_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{S_{1}\otimes S_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{O_{1}\otimes O_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{S_{1},S_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{1}\otimes u_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{lift}(e_{1})\otimes\mathsf{lift}(e_{2})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e_{1}\otimes e_{2}}

We conclude this section with an example of a tangency and the module of systems that it defines.

Example 7.15

(Tangent bundle tangency)
.

A
submersion

p\colon M\to N
of manifolds is a smooth map whose pushforward
T_{x}p:T_{x}M\to T_{p(x)}N
on tangent spaces is surjective. Submersions stable under pullback, and so equip the category
\mathsf{Man}
of manifolds with the structure of a
display map category
. Given a submersion
p\colon M\to N
, the fibers
M_{y}:=p^{-1}(y)
are also manifolds. There is an
associated fibration
is
\pi:\mathsf{Subm}\to\mathsf{Man}
which sends a submersion
p\colon M\to N
to the manifold
N
. It has a section
T\colon\mathsf{Man}\to\mathsf{Subm}
which on objects
assigns a manifold
M
to its tangent bundle
TM\to M
. Together,
(\pi,T)
is a
tangency
.

A system in the resulting module of systems then consists of a diagram of manifolds like so:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TS}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{e^{\ast}I}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces O}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}

which we interpret as follows

1.

The interface of this system is a submersion
I\to O
. The manifold
O
is a space of observations. The submersion defines a manifold
I_{o}
for every observation
o\in O
.

2.

We have a manifold
S
whose points are
states
.

3.

The map
e:S\to O
exposes an observation for every state.

4.

There are smooth maps
u:I_{e(s)}\to T_{s}S
which are organized into a map of bundles
u:e^{\ast}I\to TS
. These maps define a tangent vector
u(s,i)\in T_{s}S
parameterized by
i\in I
. These maps express the system of ordinary differential equations

\frac{ds}{dt}=u(s,t)

These systems interact through lenses which allow us to set the parameters of some systems by exposed observations of other systems.

Recall that a map out of
the timeline Moore machine
is a
trajectory
for
deterministic Moore machines
. Similarly, in this module of systems, there is a ‘clock system’ defined by the ODE
\frac{dt}{dt}=1
which is expressed as the following diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T\mathbb{R}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{R}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{R}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{R}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{R}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{R}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}}

System maps out of this clock system are solutions of the codomain system.

7.3
Doctrine of open coalgebras

In this section, we will define a
doctrine
of
open coalgebras
. Where a coalgebra for an endofunctor
F
is a map
d:S\to FS
, and open coalgebra will be a kind of
generalized Moore machine
.

Definition 7.16

(Open coalgebras of an endofunctor)
.

Let
\mathsf{C}
be a cartesian category and let
F:\mathsf{C}\to\mathsf{C}
be an endofunctor. Let
I
and
O
be objects of
\mathsf{C}
. An
open coalgebra
for
f
with interface
{I\choose O}
consists of an object
S\in C
together with functions

\left\{\begin{aligned} e&:S\to O\\
u&:S\times I\to FS\end{aligned}\right.

If
\mathsf{C}
is cartesian closed, an open coalgebra for
F
with interface
{I\choose O}
is equivalently a coalgebra for the endofunctor
X\mapsto O\times(FX)^{I}
.

We will now define a doctrine of open coalgebras.

Definition 7.17

(2-Category of endofunctors on cartesian categories)
.

Define the 2-category
\mathcal{E}\mathsf{nd}^{\mathsf{colax}}(\mathcal{C}\mathsf{art})
of
endofunctors on cartesian categories
to be the following pullback of 2-categories:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{E}\mathsf{nd}^{\mathsf{colax}}(\mathcal{C}\mathsf{art})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{2}\mathcal{C}\mathsf{at}^{\mathsf{colax}}(\mathsf{Arr},\mathcal{C}\mathsf{at})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}\mathsf{art}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}\mathsf{at}^{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(()_{0},()_{1})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta}

Explicitly:

1.

The objects of
\mathcal{E}\mathsf{nd}^{\mathsf{colax}}(\mathcal{C}\mathsf{art})
are cartesian endofunctors
F:\mathsf{C}\to\mathsf{C}
of cartesian categories
\mathsf{C}
.

2.

A morphism is a colax square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{D}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{D}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}

where
A\colon\mathsf{C}\to\mathsf{D}
is a
cartesian
functor.

3.

A 2-cell is a natural transformation
\varphi:A\Rightarrow B
to that the following equation holds:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{D}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{D}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{C}\mathsf{D}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{=}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{D}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{D}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{D}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}

Construction 7.18

(Tangency associated to an endofunctor on a cartesian category)
.

Let
F:\mathsf{C}\to\mathsf{C}
be an endofunctor of a cartesian category
\mathsf{C}
. Define the section
-\times F-:\mathsf{C}\to\mathsf{proj}_{\mathsf{C}}
of the
simple fibration
of
\mathsf{C}
by the following composite:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{C}\times\mathsf{C}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{C}\times\mathsf{C}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{proj}_{\mathsf{C}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{C}\times F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\times}

where
\mathsf{C}\times\mathsf{C}\xrightarrow{\times}\mathsf{proj}_{\mathsf{C}}
is the functor which on objects is defined by the projection

(c,x)\mapsto(c\times x\to c)

and on morphisms is defined by:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{c_{1}\times Fc_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{c_{2}\times Fc_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\alpha:c_{1}\to c_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mapsto}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{c_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{c_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha\times f\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}

This gives a cartesian 2-functor
\mathcal{E}\mathsf{nd}^{\mathsf{colax}}(\mathcal{C}\mathsf{art})\to\mathcal{T}\mathsf{an}
.

Note that it sends a 1-cell
(A,\alpha):(\mathsf{C},F)\to(\mathsf{D},G)
to the following 2-cell:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{D}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{C}\times\mathsf{C}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{D}\times\mathsf{D}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{C}\times\mathsf{C}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{D}\times\mathsf{D}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{proj}_{\mathsf{C}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{proj}_{\mathsf{D}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\times A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{C}\times F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{D}\times G}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}_{A}\times\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\times A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\times}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\times}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{proj}_{A}}

Proof.

Verification is straightforward: the 1- and 2-cells of
\mathcal{E}\mathsf{nd}^{\mathsf{colax}}(\mathcal{C}\mathsf{art})
appear in the middle of
Figure
7.18
almost entirely unaltered.∎

In the doctrine of open coalgebras, as systems theory will be a symmetric monoidal object of
\mathcal{E}\mathsf{nd}^{\mathsf{colax}}(\mathcal{C}\mathsf{art})
. By
symmetry of internalization
, it is straightforward to compute what these are.

Lemma 7.19

(Symmetric monoidal object of
\mathcal{E}\mathsf{nd}^{\mathsf{colax}}(\mathcal{C}\mathsf{art})
)
.

A symmetric monoidal object of the
2-category
\mathcal{E}\mathsf{nd}^{\mathsf{colax}}(\mathcal{C}\mathsf{art})
of endofunctors on cartesian categories
consists of a lax symmetric monoidal structure on the endofunctor
F:\mathsf{C}\to\mathsf{C}
with respect to the cartesian product of
\mathsf{C}
.

Proof.

Explicitly,
symmetry of internalization
says that a symmetric monoidal object of
\mathcal{E}\mathsf{nd}^{\mathsf{colax}}(\mathcal{C}\mathsf{art})
consists of an endofunctor
F:\mathsf{C}\to\mathsf{C}
on a cartesian category together with a symmetric monoidal structure
1:\bullet\to\mathsf{C}
and
\otimes:\times\mathsf{C}\to\mathsf{C}
— both cartesian functors — for which
F
is lax symmetric monoidal. But if
1:\bullet\to\mathsf{C}
is a cartesian functor, it must be the inclusion of the terminal object; and for
\otimes:\mathsf{C}\times\mathsf{C}\to\mathsf{C}
to be cartesian means that we have a coherent interchange isomorphism

(x\times y)\otimes(z\times w)\xrightarrow{\sim}(x\otimes z)\times(y\otimes w).

By the Eckman-Hilton argument, or more generally by the Baez-Dolan stabilization hypothesis (see Corollary 6.2.9 of
[
GH15
]
), this implies that the monoidal product and cartesian product must coincide:
\otimes\cong\times
. Therefore, the data of a symmetric monoidal object in
\mathcal{E}\mathsf{nd}^{\mathsf{colax}}(\mathcal{C}\mathsf{art})
consists of a lax monoidal structure on
F
with respect to the cartesian product of
\mathsf{C}
.∎

Definition 7.20

(Doctrine of open coalgebras)
.

We define the
doctrine
of
open coalgebras
to be the restriction of the
doctrine of generalized Moore machines
along the cartesian 2-functor
\mathcal{E}\mathsf{nd}^{\mathsf{colax}}(\mathcal{C}\mathsf{art})\to\mathcal{T}\mathsf{an}
of
Construction
7.18
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{E}\mathsf{nd}^{\mathsf{colax}}(\mathcal{C}\mathsf{art})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{T}\mathsf{an}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\ell}\mathcal{M}\mathsf{od}_{\mathsf{r}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}\mathsf{art}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}\mathsf{ib}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{D}\mathsf{bl}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{M}\mathsf{oore}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Simp}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{L}\mathsf{ens}}

where
\mathsf{Simp}:\mathcal{C}\mathsf{art}\to\mathcal{F}\mathsf{ib}
is sends a cartesian category
\mathsf{C}
to its
simple fibration

\mathsf{proj}_{\mathsf{C}}
.

Notation 7.21

(Lenses of simple fibrations)
.

Let
\mathsf{C}
be a cartesian category. An object of
\mathbb{L}\mathsf{ens}(\mathsf{proj}_{\mathsf{C}})
is a pair
{O\times I\choose O}
indicating that
O\times I
lives in the fiber over
O
in the simple fibration
\mathsf{proj}_{\mathsf{C}}
. We often abbreviate this object to simply
{I\choose O}
.

Explication 7.22

(Doctrine of open coalgebras)
.

From
Lemma
7.19
, a systems theory in the doctrine of open coalgebras consists of an endofunctor
F:\mathsf{C}\to\mathsf{C}
with a lax monoidal structure with respect to the cartesian product of
\mathsf{C}
. In the module of systems produced by this systems theory

•

Interfaces are pairs of objects in
\mathsf{C}
notated
{I\choose O}
.

•

A system with interface
{I\choose O}
is a span in
\mathsf{proj}_{\mathsf{C}}
of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{S\times FS}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{S\times I}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{O\times I}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces O}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{S}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\pi_{S},u)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e\times I}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{S}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{O}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}

which consists ultimately of maps
e:S\to O
and
u:S\times I\to FS
. This is precisely an
open coalgebra
.

•

The parallel product of open coalgebras
{u_{1}\choose e_{1}}:{FS_{1}\choose S_{1}}\mathrel{\mkern 3.0mu\vbox{\hbox{$\shortmid$}}\mkern-10.0mu{\to}}{I_{1}\choose O_{1}}
and
{u_{2}\choose e_{2}}:{FS_{2}\choose S_{2}}\mathrel{\mkern 3.0mu\vbox{\hbox{$\shortmid$}}\mkern-10.0mu{\to}}{I_{2}\choose O_{2}}
is given by
e_{1}\times e_{2}:S_{1}\times S_{2}\to O_{1}\times O_{2}
together with

(S_{1}\times S_{1})\times(I_{1}\times I_{2})\cong(S_{1}\times I_{1})\times(S_{2}\times I_{2})\xrightarrow{u_{1}\times u_{2}}FS_{1}\times FS_{2}\xrightarrow{\mu_{F}}F(S_{1}\times S_{2})

In the remainder of this section we detail examples particular systems theories in
the doctrine of open coalgebras
.

Example 7.23

(Deterministic Moore machines)
.

Under
the doctrine of open coalgebras
, the identity endomorphism on
\mathsf{Set}
is a systems theory that defines a module of determinstic Moore machines
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{L}\mathsf{ens}(\mathsf{proj}_{\mathsf{Set}})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{M}\mathsf{oore}(\mathsf{proj}_{\mathsf{Set}},(-)^{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}

via the simple fibration
\mathsf{proj}_{\mathsf{Set}}
and the tangency
(-)^{2}\colon\mathsf{Set}\to\mathsf{Set}\times\mathsf{Set}
. This module of systems is covered in detail in
Section
4.2.2
.

Example 7.24

(Non-deterministic Moore machines)
.

Under
the doctrine of open coalgebras
, the powerset monad on
\mathsf{Set}
is a systems theory that defines a module of non-determinstic Moore machines.

Example 7.25

(Partially observable Markov decision processes (POMDPs))
.

Under
the doctrine of open coalgebras
, the
Giry monad

[
Gir82
]
on measurable spaces —
f:\mathsf{Meas}\to\mathsf{Meas}
— is a systems theory that defines a module of
partially observable Markov decision process
(POMDPs).

In this module, we interpret an interface
{I\choose O}
as consisting of our set of potential
observations

O
as well as our menu of
actions

I
, and interpret a system
{u\choose e}:{fS\choose S}\mathrel{\mkern 3.0mu\vbox{\hbox{$\shortmid$}}\mkern-10.0mu{\to}}{I\choose O}
as consisting of a
deterministic
observation
e:S\to O
as well as a
stochastic
update
u:S\times I\to fS
that defines a conditional probability kernel
P(-\mid s,i):=u(s,i)
of the next state on the current state.

We can augment the Giry monad to include a reward term
\mathbb{R}
. For example, we can consider the endofunctor on measureable spaces that is defined on objects by
X\mapsto\mathbb{R}\times f(X)
. Or by
X\mapsto f(\mathbb{R}\times X)
if we want to preserve monadicity.

There are of course many different probability monads, such as the monad of finitary distributions on
\mathsf{Set}

[
Fri09
]
, the Radon monad on the category of compact Hausdorff spaces
[
Kei08
]
, the probability monad on quasi-Borel spaces
[
HKSY17
]
, and many more. All of these give specific definitions of POMDPs with particular notions of state space and probability distribution.

Example 7.26

(Other non-deterministic coalgebras)
.

There are many different sorts of non-deterministic coalgebras other than
the one captured by powerset
. For example, Fritz, Perrone, and Rezagholi
[
FPR21
]
construct a Hoare hyperspace monad on topological spaces sending a topological space to a space of its closed subsets. They also construct a probability monad on topological spaces and show that the support of a distribution gives a commutative monad morphism to the Hoare hyperspace monad. This gives an example of a morphism
F\Rightarrow G
of endofunctors which, by the pseudo-functoriality of the doctrine, gives us a compositionality theorem relating
POMDPs
(with respect to their probability monad) and
non-deterministic Moore machines
(with repsect to the Hoare hyperspace monad).

7.4
Doctrine of ODEs in tangent categories

In this section, we present a
doctrine
of ordinary differential equations in
tangent categories

[
CC13
]
, expressed as parameterized sections of tangent bundles. This expands the approach taken in Section 3.5.2 of
[
Mye21
]
.

In
[
CCGZ24
]
, Capucci, Crutwell, Ghani, and Zanasi consider a notion of
first order differentiable structures
(FODS) (Definition 58 of
[
CCGZ24
]
) in a 2-category
\mathcal{K}
and compare this notion to various notions of tangent category appearing prior in the literature. A FODS in
\mathcal{K}
consists of a fibration
\pi:E\to B
in
\mathcal{K}
with structured biproducts together with a section
T:B\to E
.

Definition 7.27

(First order differential structures (FODS))
.

Let
\mathcal{K}
be a 2-category with finite 2-limits and a finite 2-limit preserving forgetful functor
U:\mathcal{K}\to\mathcal{C}\mathsf{at}
. Define the 2-category
\mathcal{F}\mathsf{ODS}(\mathcal{K})
of
first order differential structures
in
\mathcal{K}
(following Definition 58 of
[
CCGZ24
]
) as the following pullback of 2-categories:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{F}\mathsf{ODS}(\mathcal{K})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{2}\mathcal{C}\mathsf{at}^{\mathsf{colax}}(\Delta[2],\mathcal{K})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{F}\mathsf{ib}^{+}(\mathcal{K})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{2\mathcal{C}\mathsf{at}(\Delta[1],\mathcal{C}\mathsf{at})\times\mathcal{C}\mathsf{at}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{2}\mathcal{C}\mathsf{at}^{\mathsf{colax}}(\Delta[1],\mathcal{K})^{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(d_{0}^{\ast},d_{1}^{\ast})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathsf{forget},d_{0}^{\ast})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathsf{inc},\mathrm{id})}

where
\mathcal{F}\mathsf{ib}^{+}(\mathcal{K})
is the 2-category of fibrations with biproducts in
\mathcal{K}
.

Remark 7.28

(Remark on tangency morphisms and tangent categories)
.

While morphisms of FODS aren’t considered in
[
CCGZ24
]
, Lanfranchi does define a 2-category of tangent categories and “lax” morphisms in
[
Lan25
]
(see Definitions 2.5, 2.7 and 2.10 of
[
Lan25
]
). What Lanfranchi calls a “lax” morphism of tangent categories (Definition 2.7 of
[
Lan25
]
) corresponds to
our (colax) morphisms of tangencies
when considered as a FODS by Theorem 56 of
[
CCGZ24
]
. For this reason, we feel justified in using our morphisms of tangencies as those of tangent categories or FODS more generally.

In
[
Leu17
]
, Leung shows (Theorem 14.1 of
[
Leu17
]
) that a tangent category structure on a category
C
is equivalent to a pseudo-monoidal functor
\mathsf{Weil}_{1}\to\mathsf{End}(C)
from the monoidal category of first order Weil algebras satisfying two limit preservation properties. Such a monoidal functor corresponds to a pseudofunctor
\mathcal{B}\mathsf{Weil}_{1}\to\mathcal{C}\mathsf{at}
. The morphisms Lanfranchi consideres in Definition 2.7 of
[
Lan25
]
should correspond to
colax
morphisms of these pseudo-functors.

A core idea of
[
Lan25
]
is that Leung’s tangent structures as pseudo-functors
\mathcal{B}\mathsf{Weil}_{1}\to\mathcal{C}\mathsf{at}
can be thought of as generalized monads (themselves functors
\mathcal{B}\Delta_{+}\to\mathcal{C}\mathsf{at}
). Colax morphisms of monads are the kind for which the Kleisli construction is functorial; colax morphisms of tangent categories (what Lanfranchi unfortunately calls "lax") are the kind for which vector fields are functorial. This is ultimately why our use of
colax morphisms of tangencies
is the correct choice for understanding the compositionality of systems of ODEs.

Next we will see that if
\mathcal{K}
is a concrete 2-category with a finite 2-limit preserving forgetful 2-functor
U:\mathcal{K}\to\mathcal{C}\mathsf{at}
, then a FODS will induce a
tangency
.

Definition 7.29

(Doctrine of ODEs in first order differential structures)
.

Let
\mathcal{K}
be a concrete 2-category with a finite 2-limit preserving forgetful 2-functor
U:\mathcal{K}\to\mathcal{C}\mathsf{at}
. The forgetful functor
U:\mathcal{K}\to\mathcal{C}\mathsf{at}
determines a square of cartesian 2-functors:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{F}\mathsf{ODS}(\mathcal{K})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{T}\mathsf{an}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{F}\mathsf{ib}^{+}(\mathcal{K})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}\mathsf{ib}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}

which when post composing with the
doctrine of systems in a tangency
gives us a
doctrine
of
ordinary differential equations
in a first order differential structure:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{F}\mathsf{ODS}(\mathcal{K})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{T}\mathsf{an}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\ell}\mathcal{M}\mathsf{od}_{\mathsf{r}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{F}\mathsf{ib}^{+}(\mathcal{K})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}\mathsf{ib}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{D}\mathsf{bl}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{M}\mathsf{oore}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{L}\mathsf{ens}}

There are many examples of FODS, tangent categories, and cartesian differential categories. Each of these is a systems theory that will produce a module of systems whose systems are generalized ordinary differential equations expressed as a parameterized section of a tangent bundle:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TS}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{e^{\ast}I}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces O}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{lift}(e)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}

Example 7.30

(Euclidean ODEs)
.

Let
\mathsf{Euc}
be the category whose objects are natural numbers
n
and whose maps are smooth functions
\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{m}
. The category
\mathsf{Euc}
is a
cartesian differential category
, which are an example of a first-order differential structure
[
CCGZ24
]
. Explicitly, the underlying fibration of the FODS induced by
\mathsf{Euc}
is the
simple fibration
associated to
\mathsf{Euc}
. The section of the FODS induced by
\mathsf{Euc}
on objects sends
\mathbb{R}^{n}
to
\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
, and on morphisms sends
f:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{m}
to its derivative

\displaystyle\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}

\displaystyle\to\mathbb{R}^{m}\times\mathbb{R}^{m}

\displaystyle(x,v)

\displaystyle\mapsto(f(x),D_{x}f(v))

The FODS induced by
\mathsf{Euc}
is a systems theory which produces a module of systems whose:

•

Interfaces consist of numbers
n_{o}
and
n_{i}
of exposed variable and parameters, respectively.

•

Systems consist of:

–

A number
n_{s}
of
state variables
.

–

A map
\vec{e}:\mathbb{R}^{n_{s}}\to\mathbb{R}^{n_{o}}
exposing
n_{o}
variables of state.

–

A map
\vec{u}:\mathbb{R}^{n_{s}}\times\mathbb{R}^{n_{i}}\to\mathbb{R}^{n_{s}}
giving a vector of state displacement vectors, representing the system of ODEs

\frac{d\vec{x}}{dt}=\vec{u}(\vec{x},\vec{i}).

8
Restricting doctines to free interactions

In
Section
5
,
Section
6
, and
Section
7
, we constructed a number of examples of doctrines. However, the resulting modules of systems produced by these doctrines were over double categories of interactions which were qualitatively complex, often including the systems and system maps themselves as special cases. therefore, composing systems theories through these interactions could have arbitarily complex effects. For example:

1.

The were loose right modules on the double category of
all
(co)spans in
\mathsf{C}
. These (co)spans could include arbitary component systems; they certainly do more than
just
compose.

2.

The
systems theories of generalized Moore machines associated to tangencies
are acted on by all
generalized lenses
; these do more than compose, since they can allow arbitrary intermediate transformations of the outputs and parameters of the Moore machines.

In this section, we will show how to restrict these more general interactions to simpler
coupling schemes
or
wiring diagrams
. We base our approach on the following observation.

Observation 8.1

(Wiring diagrams are free interactions)
.

A wiring diagram for a given
doctrine
is an interaction in a free theory of interactions in that doctrine.

8.1
Wiring diagrams are free interactions

Recall that given a
doctrine
is a commuting diagram as follows.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{D}\mathsf{}_{\mathsf{sys}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\ell}\mathcal{M}\mathsf{od}_{\mathsf{r}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{D}\mathsf{}_{\mathsf{inter}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{D}\mathsf{bl}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{S}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\mathcal{D}\mathsf{}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(-)_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{I}}

Given a systems theory
T\in\mathcal{D}\mathsf{}_{\mathsf{sys}}
, we call
\pi_{\mathcal{D}\mathsf{}}(T)
the
interaction theory
. Under
\mathbb{I}
,
T
produces a double category of interactions which acts on the systems in the modulee of systems defined by
T
. The loose arrows of this double category of interactions are what we term
interactions
. In the literature, many interactions are in the form of
wiring diagrams
and that these are freely generated interactions (see
[
Spi13
]
,
[
VSL14
]
,
[
LBPF22
]
).

Let’s see
Observation
8.1
borne out in a few special cases.

Example 8.2

(Undirected wiring diagrams are free interactions)
.

Systems in the are well known to form
hypergraph categories
, which by the work of Fong and Spivak
[
FS18b
]
are equivalently described as algebras for operads of cospans of typed finite sets (see
[
FS18b
]
for a review of hypergraph categories and how they arise as (co)spans and (co)relations).

If
X
is a set of "types", then the category of typed finite sets is defined to be the slice category
